new Edge-Efficient Joint RAW Image Compression and Restoration for Smart Wearable Devices

Authors: Yunong Sun, Sicheng Chen, Fei Xia

Abstract: Smart wearable devices, such as augmented reality glasses, increasingly rely on cameras to understand and interact with their surroundings. However, their compact sensors often capture noisy RAW images, while limited compute and power make high-quality on-device restoration difficult. This creates an emerging need to reduce both local computation and transmission cost without sacrificing image quality. Two common solutions are to offload restoration or to compress the raw data before transmission. The first makes RAW transmission costly; the second reduces the payload but can spend bitrate preserving sensor noise. We address this fundamental mismatch with an asymmetric framework for joint RAW compression and restoration that directly optimizes the transmitted representation for clean image reconstruction. A lightweight on-device encoder compresses noisy RAW measurements into a bitstream, while a higher-capacity backend decoder jointly denoises and demosaics the encoded representation. We use knowledge distillation to train a smaller encoder for tighter compute budgets. Experiments on real and synthetic data show improved rate-restoration performance over strong cascaded compression-restoration pipelines and learned compression baselines. On SIDD, our method improves PSNR by more than 1.5 dB over cascaded baselines at approximately 0.25 bits per pixel. We further validate the method on real 12 MP RAW images captured with commercial smart glasses and measure sender-side latency on both smartphone and smart-glasses hardware. The distilled encoder achieves a 27.6$\times$ NPU inference speedup over on-device NAFNet for 256$\times$256 output patches. The code is available at https://github.com/coolsyn2000/CRAW

URLs: https://github.com/coolsyn2000/CRAW

new ReCoCo: From Patches to Compositions for Visual Representation Learning

Authors: Juan Guti\'errez, Saman Zadtootaghaj, Jos\'e Luis Blanco-Murillo, Sara Atito

Abstract: Self-supervised learning has produced powerful visual representations, yet learning strong dense features remains challenging. While consistency between augmented views provides strong image-level supervision, extending this principle to local visual content is less straightforward. Existing approaches often match individual patches across views or identify coherent regions, introducing uncertainties in correspondence estimation and assumptions about how visual content should be grouped. We introduce ReCoCo (Regional Composition Consistency), a principled approach that extends cross-view consistency to the regional level without requiring such correspondences or semantic grouping. ReCoCo represents the content shared between augmented views through compositions of their patch-level predictions over learned visual prototypes. By aligning these compositions, the model learns from regions containing multiple visual concepts without requiring them to be separated beforehand. ReCoCo integrates naturally with teacher-student self-distillation and introduces no additional learned parameters. Extensive experiments demonstrate substantial improvements in frozen-feature segmentation, clustering, and regional retrieval while maintaining competitive image-level recognition, alleviating the trade-off between dense and global representation quality.

new Does the Action Matter? Probing Action-Conditioned Semantic Forecasting as a World Model for Autonomous Driving

Authors: Binay Kumar Singh, Niels Da Vitoria Lobo

Abstract: World models are increasingly used in autonomous driving to predict how a scene evolves under the ego vehicle's actions, supporting counterfactual reasoning and model-based planning. However, existing evaluations focus on prediction fidelity and planning performance, and rarely verify that the predictions depend on the conditioning action. In this paper, we study action dependence in action-conditioned semantic forecasting, which predicts future semantic segmentation from past segmentations and the ego action. We compare a forecaster with feature-wise and input-level action conditioning against an identical model without action input, and measure action dependence with test-time action interventions scored by standard segmentation metrics. The evaluation is validated in a synthetic environment with known action-dependent dynamics and applied to Cityscapes and BDD100K, both within each dataset and across the two vehicle platforms. Experiments show that the executed action improves mid-term mIoU by at most 2.5 points on Cityscapes and 0.6 points on BDD100K, whereas a wrong action reduces it by up to 23.2 and 7.0 points, below the model without action input. On real data, feature-wise conditioning depends 1.6 times more on the action than input-level conditioning at similar accuracy, whereas both depend on it equally in the synthetic environment, where the history does not determine the action. These results show that an accuracy gain indicates that a driving world model uses its action, but not how strongly its forecasts depend on it. Code is available at https://github.com/binayksingh/does-action-matter.

URLs: https://github.com/binayksingh/does-action-matter.

new Gaspard: Expressive unified speech and gesture synthesis with continuous autoregressive flow-matching and decoupled classifier-free guidance

Authors: T\'eo Guichoux, Th\'eodor Lemerle, Catherine Pelachaud, Nicolas Obin

Abstract: Human communication involves highly coordinated multimodal signals such as speech and co-speech gestures. Existing approaches for generating both modalities commonly follow a cascaded pipeline, where speech is generated first and subsequently used to drive gesture generation; speech generation is therefore independent of gestures. We introduce Gaspard, a unified speech--gesture generation model based on continuous autoregressive generation with next-step flow-matching predictions, and trained on Seamless Interaction, a large-scale multimodal dataset. The heterogeneous temporal properties of speech and gesture motivate modality-specific prediction heads and decoupled classifier-free guidance over the two generation streams. This guidance rebalances the influence of speech and gesture at inference time, improving expressivity while mitigating dynamics collapse in continuous autoregressive gesture generation. Objective and perceptual evaluations show that Gaspard is a strong gesture generation model against unimodal baselines and that it produces highly synchronous speech and gestures compared to a matched cascaded system.

new NereidVLA: Learning to Understand and Navigate the Underwater World

Authors: Chenjie Zhao, Zhiliang Zhu, Quan Chen, Yuquan Xue, Xingxue Shi, Puyue Wang, Zhiqiang Zhao, Kangye Zheng, Boyang Xu, Mingwei Lin, Dejun Li

Abstract: Underwater navigation requires translating visual spatial cues into motion, yet marine vision-language datasets rarely include paired robot trajectories. We present NereidVLA, a vision-language-action model that transfers marine spatial knowledge to language-conditioned 3D navigation. We first train Sea2Know on approximately two million marine examples to learn target grounding, spatial relations, and scene structure without paired robot-motion supervision. We then adapt it using expert trajectories executed under simulated underwater vehicle dynamics. Given RGB histories and a language instruction, NereidVLA predicts discrete motion tokens that decode into local 3D trajectories for closed-loop tracking. Evaluation combines an independent six-dimensional underwater navigation understanding benchmark with offline prediction and closed-loop navigation. Under matched navigation training and execution settings, marine spatial pretraining increases simulation success from 72.4% to 84.4%. Without fine-tuning on the real test environment, NereidVLA achieves 78.75% success in laboratory-pool robot trials, compared with 47.50% for InternVLA-N1. These results show that marine spatial supervision can support downstream motion learning and physical underwater navigation.

new ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection

Authors: Shagun Mittal, Raymond A. Yeh, Satish Ukkusuri

Abstract: Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.

new PrismRestore: A Compact Restoration Toolbox for Agentic Image Restoration

Authors: Zhaocheng Yu, Kui Jiang, Jing Cao, Yiang Chen, Xiaocheng Feng, Junjun Jiang, Xianming Liu

Abstract: Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. We propose PrismRestore, which constructs a compact bank of callable restoration actions from a single frozen all-in-one model. Our approach builds on task-dependent yet overlapping channel responses in the shared model. An offline task-prior search uses activation and gradient statistics to group channels with similar response profiles, then searches task-specific modulation coefficients over non-representative channels while leaving representative channels unmodulated. The resulting prior bank enables explicit action selection and composition without additional restoration backbones or task-specific adapter modules. A state-conditioned policy learns to compose these actions using Group Relative Policy Optimization. Its objective separately normalizes fidelity and perceptual feedback within groups of trajectories sampled for the same input, and combines terminal evaluation with return-to-go derived from stepwise quality changes. Experiments on synthetic composite-degradation and real-world multi-task benchmarks show improvements over the evaluated all-in-one baselines and restoration quality competitive with multi-model agents. PrismRestore achieves these results with a single restoration backbone and faster inference than the evaluated agentic baselines.

new Unified Law for Visual Tasks (The Four Laws of Computer Vision)

Authors: Sen Fang, Yalin Feng, Yancheng Zhang, Yanxin Zhang, Zonglin Lyu, Hongbin Zhong, Liwei Che, Rui Wu, Chen Chen, Dimitris N. Metaxas

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a Unified Law for Visual Tasks, which could be further divided into Four Laws of Computer Vision, with the goal of enabling a single framework or method to uniformly train on and perform nearly all visual tasks. Our starting point is an inspiration and hypothesis: if the world information is encoded into image, which we call World Snapshots, then image processing is world processing, and image generation is World Snapshot generation. Furthermore, if all visual and visualization tasks can be reformulated as generation tasks, then a unified method, World Snapshot Models (WSMs), can perform the vast majority of visual tasks, much as large language models have largely unified natural language processing. This paper validates these laws across more than 1,000 trained and evaluated visual tasks (defined by distinct input-output mappings) and presents a unified training framework for future visual-task research and applications.

new UltraTex: Aligning Physical Knowledge for High-Fidelity PBR Texture Generation

Authors: Jingzhi Bao, Xiaohe Ma, Hong Li, Yuanming Hu, Xiaoguang Han

Abstract: Generating physically based rendering (PBR) texture maps for a 3D mesh from a single reference image is highly ambiguous, as the observed appearance entangles material identity, surface reflectance, and illumination. While current generative methods produce realistic albedo maps, they still struggle to infer accurate metallic and roughness attributes for relighting. We present UltraTex, a cascaded multi-view framework that moves beyond appearance matching by integrating physical knowledge into multi-view diffusion-based PBR generation. UltraTex first jointly predicts albedo and world-space shading normals to generate more detailed albedo maps, and then predicts metallic-roughness (MR) maps conditioned on the generated albedo, reference image, and material semantic priors. To inject material semantics, a lightweight adapter transforms hidden-layer features of a vision-language model (VLM) into intrinsic material representation tokens, providing part-level knowledge anchors for MR estimation. To model material-light interaction, photometric representation alignment guides intermediate diffusion features toward ground-truth material renderings under controlled lighting. This makes metallic and roughness variations observable through highlight intensity, specular sharpness, and local reflectance contrast, providing stronger guidance for fine-grained material prediction without explicit lighting supervision. Extensive experiments show that UltraTex generates high-quality textures and outperforms existing open-source and commercial methods in visual fidelity, material accuracy, and physical plausibility.

cross torchtest2: One-Call Sanity Checks for PyTorch Models,Evaluated on Real and Unseen Architectures

Authors: Grzegorz Gwardys

Abstract: Many bugs in deep learning code are silent: a layer that is never called, an optimizer created before the classification head was replaced, a recurrent layer that treats the batch as time, or an ignored attention mask. The code runs, the loss decreases somewhat, and the defect surfaces, if ever, after days of GPU time. We present torchtest2, an open-source library that runs 18 sanity checks on a single batch with one call, restores the PyTorch state it touches, and adapts to model families through plugins. We evaluate it by mutation analysis with 17 classes of silent bugs on 103 models from torchvision, timm and Hugging Face transformers, on 600 random architectures and on 20 hand-written realistic bugs, against the checks of the original torchtest, overfitting a single batch, a smoke test and a training monitor in the style of AUTOTRAINER and DeepDiagnosis. Every mutant is compared with its healthy counterpart, and intervals come from a bootstrap over models. After freezing the tool, we ran it on 191 models of architecture families it had never seen: counting, for every tool alike, a failing report where the healthy model's report does not fail, it detected 78% of the injected bugs (95% CI 75-80), against 40% for the original torchtest checks and 48% for the training monitor. On real models its batch-independence check found a bug in timm's GCViT that couples the images of a batch. Real models also exposed false alarms that neither the tool's own test suite nor random architectures had revealed. Two independent collections of real silent bugs mark the limits of the approach: only 3 of 28 reproduced PyTorch bugs from Stack Overflow and 3 of the 18 silent training errors released with TrainCheck are within reach of a pre-training model tester. A median check takes 1.0 s. We release the library, the benchmark and all raw results.

cross Asymmetric Optical Image Watermarking

Authors: Guang Yang

Abstract: Watermark detectors held by a provider cannot be checked by third parties, and a party that can detect a mark can usually also forge it. We study a two-layer image watermark in which the Fourier transform at the core of the pipeline is performed by a coherent optical 4F unit. Layer 1 carries an Ed25519 signature bound to the image content in Fourier magnitudes, so anyone with the public key verifies offline. Layer 2 is a key-defined carrier written by a secret phase hologram and read by a matched-filter correlator. The optics is a scalar-diffraction simulation with finite modulator bits, pixel aperture, pupil, defocus, misalignment, and sensor noise. No hardware is built. All criteria were fixed in a hashed specification before any optical result. The simulated optical signer reproduces the digital pipeline (field error 1.7e-15, bit agreement 99.997%), verifies at least 99.5% of 877 images under each of 8 post-sensor settings, one unattacked, and accepts none of 40,670 unmarked images. The optical carrier is key-selective: the matched key detects it on 99.8% of the unattacked images, and detection falls to 0% under a scale error of 1% and to 0% under a mask shift of 1 bin. Security rests on the signature and the content binding, for which we separate what is provable from what is measured.

cross Streaming Generative Video Compression with Embedding Alignment and In-Context Propagation

Authors: Zhitao Wang, Hengyu Man, Han Liu, Wenrui Li, Debin Zhao, Xiaopeng Fan

Abstract: Video compression at ultra-low bitrates presents substantial challenges in preserving perceptual quality. Although pretrained video diffusion transformers (DiTs) provide powerful generative priors, existing generative video compression (GVC) methods built on them typically rely on computationally expensive VAE decoders to reconstruct video frames. Moreover, jointly refining multiple frames to exploit the inherent spatiotemporal modeling capabilities of video DiTs introduces additional buffering delay. To address these challenges, we propose SGVC, a streaming generative video compression framework that adapts a pretrained latent video DiT into a causal pixel-space refiner. Specifically, SGVC projects decoded features from a contextual pixel codec directly into the DiT input embedding space. To bridge the representation gap between codec-derived embeddings and pretrained DiT embeddings, we introduce Embedding Alignment, which aligns the projected embeddings with input embeddings extracted from the original frames during training. The refined DiT hidden states are then mapped directly to the pixel domain through a lightweight reconstruction path, bypassing the pretrained VAE decoder. To further exploit the spatiotemporal priors of the video DiT while maintaining causal decoding, we introduce In-Context Propagation, which reuses layer-wise K/V states across frames to exploit historical generative context, improving temporal consistency without requiring clip-wise buffering. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SGVC achieves competitive rate-perception performance, with average BD-rate savings of 71.97% over S$^2$VC in terms of DISTS. Project Page: https://chigland.github.io/SGVC/

URLs: https://chigland.github.io/SGVC/

cross Lossless Reversible Watermarking for AIGC with Applications in Authentication

Authors: Yupeng Qiu, Han Fang, Ee-Chien Chang

Abstract: AIGC watermarking is commonly used for tracing, but it can also support applications such as authentication and tampering detection. We study lossless reversible watermarking, which enables exact recovery of the original watermarked image after permissible distortions. By exploiting the relatively low entropy of the information required for image generation, together with robust latent-space watermarking, we design a framework that preserves sufficient information for lossless recovery. We then apply the framework to AIGC authentication, which generally faces two key challenges. First, the authentication mechanism must resist adaptive adversaries. Second, the content relevant to authentication, such as visual designs or expressed messages, may depend on application requirements that are not known at generation time. To solve these difficulties, we incorporate cryptographic message authentication to provide unforgeability against adaptive attacks. Lossless recovery provides the original image as reference evidence, after which a suitable analyzer can be selected to make the authentication decision. Authors: Jiarui Cui, Boxun Yan

Abstract: While multimodal language models can interpret diagrams and generate structured outputs, their capacity to connect passive radio-frequency (RF) design intent, layout geometry, and electromagnetic behavior remains largely untested. cross LIC-Audit: How Comparable Are Reported Gains in Learned Image Compression?

Authors: Haisheng Fu, Jie Liang, Runyu Yang, Feng Ding, Xin Dong, Zixiao Jin, Jingning Han

Abstract: Learned image compression (LIC) papers often report BD-rate improvements of only a few percent. They are measured against VTM reference software whose version keeps changing: the claims we audit name 7 versions, 5 in 2025 papers alone. But how comparable are these numbers across papers, and what evidence should future papers give for such small gains? We introduce LIC-Audit, an analysis of 353 released rate-distortion curves from 36 repositories, and a controlled re-evaluation of eight public codec releases with 28,193 encode-decode records. We identify three problems. First, different VTM reference runs change BD-rates by up to 3.56 percentage points, and on CLIC Pro published numbers order 10 of 55 method pairs differently from a common computation; comparing codecs directly over a fixed quality range removes this dependence. Second, releases use different and mostly undocumented conventions for counting bits, computing PSNR, and padding images. Under consistent measurement, the GLIC method's 1.00% lead over the HPCM method on Tecnick becomes a 1.43% deficit, changing only the padding reverses the comparison of HPCM and CMIC, and most gaps narrow when MS-SSIM replaces PSNR. Third, average curves hide how much results vary from image to image, so whether a small gain is statistically significant depends on the assumed variation. An optimistic assumption calls 12.0% of comparisons between equally good codecs significant on test sets as small as Kodak, instead of 5%; a cautious one leaves 18 of 45 differences between recent codecs undecided, yet per-image results show 12 of them to be significant. Beyond auditing past results, LIC-Audit provides a versioned evaluation protocol, a public leaderboard website with a scoring tool, source-linked benchmark data, per-image measurements, and reporting guidelines, to help future LIC papers make comparable and verifiable claims of progress.

cross Gaussian Splatoms: Direct Atomic Structure Identification in Electron Tomography

Authors: Nalini Singh, Tiffany Chien, Cedric Lim, Arthur R. C. McCray, Colin Ophus, Laura Waller

Abstract: Atomic electron tomography (AET) computationally reconstructs the 3D atomic structure of materials from a series of 2D projection measurements acquired at different tilt angles. Inspired by Gaussian splatting techniques from computer vision, we introduce an atom-centric parameterization for modeling AET samples. Instead of a conventional volumetric representation with a grid of voxels, we model the sample as a list of atoms, where each atom is a 3D Gaussian characterized by learned location, scale, and amplitude. The Gaussian model is a natural fit for atoms that allows us to easily incorporate prior information grounded in physics; we impose isotropy of atoms, a minimum interatomic distance, and realistic atomic radii. We show in simulation that our method is more robust than volumetric baselines to real-world imaging challenges, including a limited number of measurements and low electron dose. We further show that our method yields a more physically plausible reconstruction of an experimental 23,000 atom iron-platinum nanoparticle than a previously published volumetric reconstruction. Beyond tomography, our representation is a promising tool for any atomic resolution imaging modality (e.g. multislice electron ptychography).

cross Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

cross SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs

Authors: Xi Ding, Jiawei Zhang

Abstract: Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.

cross BOS Summary: The First Token Matters

Authors: Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Deng Pan, Arun Ross, Dongxiao Zhu

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) and language models (LLMs) often exhibit attention sinks, whereby particular positions, such as the beginning-of-sequence (BOS) token, receive a disproportionate share of attention. Under standard causal masking, however, the BOS query attends only to itself, so the representation that later tokens repeatedly access carries no information about the input. We introduce BOS Summary, a training-free inference-time method that repurposes this persistent pathway to carry input context. At a single transformer layer during prefill, BOS Summary relaxes the causal constraint for the BOS query row alone, letting BOS aggregate all visual and textual input tokens into a self-derived context representation while every other token retains standard causal attention. The enriched representation then remains accessible to the model throughout decoding, with no additional parameters, auxiliary modules, or decoding-time overhead. Across multiple VLM and LLM families and eight multimodal and text-only benchmarks, BOS Summary improves over direct and Chain-of-Thought prompting and outperforms recent sink-intervention methods on multi-step reasoning tasks. A mechanistic analysis shows that making BOS input-specific also weakens its sink dominance and redistributes attention toward content-bearing prompt tokens, indicating that changing what a sink carries reshapes how later layers allocate attention.

cross RageVLA: Augmentation-Trained Perception and Action for State-of-the-Art Robustness on LIBERO

Authors: Amrit Lal Singh, Smarak Kanjilal

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models look saturated on LIBERO, yet most of them fall apart on LIBERO-PRO as soon as an object moves or the instruction asks for something new. Two further tests on LIBERO-Object show that the policy acts on what it perceives. When the object positions it is given are shuffled while the camera images stay correct, its success falls from 0.965 to 0.215. When the target object is displaced by 28 cm, it still succeeds in 0.865 of the episodes, where $\pi_{0.5}$, run through the same harness, succeeds in 0.105.

cross Stability of Parallel Transport Convolution under Riemannian Metric Perturbations

Authors: Hongnian Huang, Andrew Stump

Abstract: Geometric convolutions on Riemannian manifolds depend on the geometry of the underlying domain, making stability under metric perturbations a fundamental question in geometric deep learning. This paper studies this question for parallel transport convolution through methods from differential geometry and analysis. On a fixed compact manifold, we establish that, for a continuous input signal and a continuous reference kernel supported in a sufficiently small normal neighborhood, the convolution output depends continuously in $L^2$ on the Riemannian metric in the $C^2$-topology, under the specified localization and transport-path conventions. The analysis develops perturbation estimates for geodesics, parallel transport, Jacobi fields, and exponential maps, and uses these estimates to compare the transported kernels. A central difficulty is the cut locus, which obstructs uniform comparison of the transport construction over the entire manifold. We address this difficulty by combining uniform estimates away from the cut locus with integral bounds on neighborhoods of arbitrarily small volume, while also controlling the change in the Riemannian volume measure. The resulting qualitative stability theorem connects perturbation theory for Riemannian geometric constructions with the analysis of spatial convolution operators in geometric deep learning.

cross Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients for Stable Feature Attribution

Authors: Rukmangadh Sai Myana, Sumit Kumar Jha, Yanzhao Wu

Abstract: Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients (IG) are widely used to interpret neural networks, yet imperceptible input perturbations can drastically alter their attribution maps while leaving model predictions unchanged. We introduce Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients (ERIG), a path-based attribution method that intrinsically attenuates gradient irregularities along the integration path using maximum-entropy softmax weighting with temperature parameter $\tau$. We quantify the effect of the entropy-regularized weighting on attribution stability by using the Lipschitz properties of the model and its gradients. We decompose the attribution change under an input perturbation into a model-response term, whose bound is $\tau$-independent, and a weight-variation term, whose bound decreases at rate $\mathcal{O}(1/(2\tau))$. Their ratio $R(\tau)$ characterizes the trade-off between gradient information and attribution stability. We further add a new dimension to attribution evaluation by adapting classical noise estimators into two metrics, LINV-C and LINV-P, that quantify attribution noise. Across eleven CNN and Transformer backbones on ImageNet and Oxford-IIIT Pet datasets, our experiments show that, relative to the next-best method, ERIG remains comparable on ranking-based fidelity metrics while improving localization by approximately 24% and attribution stability by approximately 47-fold on average, reaching up to four orders of magnitude improvement over IG, all at negligible additional computational cost.

cross How Uncertain Is My Action? Self-Correcting Visual Planning with Frozen World Models

Authors: Siqing Li, Luming Yang, Yijia Jiao, Junze Deng, Alper Yilmaz

Abstract: A frozen latent world model combined with sampling-based model predictive control is a visual planner. At deployment, the executed action can depart from the commanded one, and the frozen model has no way to notice. We show that the model can be run in reverse: for any hypothesized actuation shift, it predicts what it should have observed under that shift, and comparing this prediction with what was actually observed quantifies the shift and lets the planner correct itself. Unlike the residual energy, which mixes the shift with the model's own error, the score keeps only the part of the residual that a shift can produce. This shift score detects the shift with a locally optimal test, estimates it with Cramer-Rao error bars, names the dominant generator, and compensates the planner once an alarm fires. On PushT, TwoRoom and OGBench-Cube with a frozen latent world model, measured detection follows the prediction to within 0.05 AUROC. Self-correction raises success from 0.650 to 0.862 on PushT and from 0.378 to 0.432 on the harder Cube task, levels with an oracle that is told the true shift at the same block, better than five common test-time adaptation methods. On a real robotic arm, the score identifies a base-joint offset that no joint encoder registers and reduces the reach error from 36 mm to 3-5 mm, and under shifts drawn at random and sealed before the session, estimates recover 92-94% of the error reduction achieved with the true shift. The broader implication is that a frozen world model can act as a filter by predicting the future, testing action-interface uncertainty and validity, and finally correcting failures.

cross AdaS-ViT: Test-Time Adaptive Routing for Spiking Vision Transformers with Ultra-High Energy Efficiency

Authors: Zecheng Hao, Zihan Huang, Shenghao Xie, Jiyuan Zhang, Zhaofei Yu, Tiejun Huang

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) promise event-driven and energy-efficient vision, yet existing spiking vision transformers (S-ViTs) largely confine spiking tokens to fixed and uniform firing schemes along the temporal, spatial and channel dimensions, whereas suppressing the computational redundancy thus incurred calls for adaptive firing mechanisms that operate jointly across all three axes at test time. cross Geometry-bridge flow matching for blind rigid-motion correction in head cone-beam CT

Authors: Sungho Yun, Seungryong Cho

Abstract: Rigid motion of a patient head during head cone-beam CT (CBCT) scan can degrade image quality and complicate recovery of accurate anatomical structures from the measured projections. We present a 3D flow-matching prior approach that learns the image changes induced by motion. Its geometry-bridge connects motion-corrupted and motion-free Feldkamp-Davis-Kress (FDK) reconstructed images of the same source volume through progressively reduced simulated motion. The forward projector's geometry derivative supplies analytic velocity targets along this path. A plug-and-play loop encompasses the learned image updates together with spline-based pose estimation, conjugate-gradient data consistency, and image total-variation regularization. Both the refined image volume and the FDK reconstructed image volume at the estimated poses are evaluated. On simulated 360-view scans from 30 held-out CQ500 patients with nominal per-component motion amplitudes within 10 mm / 10 degrees, the framework achieved state-of-the-art image reconstruction quality and motion accuracy in comparison with other methos such as learned autofocus and diffusion-based joint reconstruction. Mean peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) reached 37.1 dB and mean detector-plane reprojection error (RPE) was 0.27 mm. With matched endpoints at the inference settings, the geometry bridge improved PSNR by 1.6 dB and reduced RPE by 33% relative to image-linear training. With 50 prior updates and coarse-to-fine motion estimation, total processing time averaged 9.1 minutes per patient on an RTX A6000.

cross RoboBuilder: Agentic Robot Co-Design with Real-world Video Feedback in the Loop

Authors: Ghadi Nehme, Dahyun Nam, Akshay Govind Srinivasan, Faez Ahmed

Abstract: Can AI agents design robots that work in the real world? We introduce RoboBuilder, a benchmark in which coding agents co-design a robot's hardware and program from standard parts, motors and a custom 3D-printed part, and every design that passes in simulation is built and tested on a physical course. Simulation alone is not enough: under public part data, none of 15 initial passing designs crossed the real course, so an agent learns the simulator from measurements it requests and videos of real runs. Across four frontier coding agents, ten obstacles at three difficulty levels, 16,427 simulations of 12,774 distinct robots and 135 physical robots built in an estimated 240 hours, agents solve nearly all easy obstacles and invent mechanisms our search baselines do not reach, but falter at harder levels, when one robot must cross every obstacle, and in the real world, where 66% of simulated passes hold across all levels. Closing the loop helps: shown a video of its real failure, an agent redesigns the robot, sometimes correcting its simulator, and removes 72% of level-1 and level-2 failures. RoboBuilder measures progress toward agents that engineer real machines. Project Page: https://ghadinehme.com/robobuilder.github.io/

URLs: https://ghadinehme.com/robobuilder.github.io/

cross Coverage Governs Accuracy: When to Expand the Lexicon and When to Fall Back in Low-Resource Manchu OCR

Authors: Ruixin Yu, Zifan Wang, Mingxin Ding, Xiaoxin Sun, Dongjie Wang, Suhua Wang, Minghao Yin

Abstract: Character-level OCR for low-resource Manchu often produces near-miss spelling errors. Lexicon-constrained decoding can correct such errors with lexical knowledge, but a fixed dictionary cannot cover the many unseen word forms produced by agglutinative word formation. We study whether morphological expansion alleviates this limitation, and how the expansion size and confidence fallback affect recognition. We segment the training transcriptions with a dictionary-based stem list, extract roots and suffix chains from them, generate about 510k candidates, select the expansion size through frequency ranking on the validation set, and decompose accuracy under hard lexicon constraints into the product of coverage and in-lexicon precision. Across three CRNN training seeds, adding 250k candidates to a base dictionary of about 29k words raises the type accuracy on unseen combinations of seen roots from 63.6% to 86.3%, and overall token accuracy from 82.4% to 84.2%. Adding all candidates lowers overall token accuracy to 81.5%, showing that the coverage gain comes with a loss of in-lexicon precision. A further comparison shows that the base dictionary with confidence fallback performs best overall among the CRNN systems tested, reaching 76.1% type and 85.5% token accuracy and outperforming the expanded lexicon both with and without fallback. A Transformer recognizer shows similar trends. These results indicate that morphological expansion relieves the constraint of a fixed dictionary, but a larger lexicon does not guarantee better recognition; in the present low-resource Manchu OCR setting, a selective combination of reliable lexical constraints and open recognition is more effective.

cross Training-Free Domain Generation for One-Shot Federated Learning with Server-Side Pre-trained Diffusion Models

Authors: Yixuan Tong, Yuanguo Bi, Mingjian Zhi, Wenchao Xu, Yuan Wu

Abstract: One-shot federated learning (OSFL) has attracted increasing attention for its ability to substantially reduce communication overhead by limiting client-server interaction to a single communication round. Nevertheless, this limited interaction makes learning from heterogeneous client distributions challenging. Diffusion-based OSFL approaches seek to address this challenge by synthesizing data that approximate client distributions. However, existing diffusion-based approaches often impose substantial computational costs on clients and lack effective visual guidance to generate data aligned with client distributions. To address these limitations, we propose FedDoG, a training-free Domain Generation framework that leverages server-side pre-trained diffusion models to synthesize data aligned with client domains. FedDoG extracts compact domain representations using a frozen image encoder, substantially reducing client-side computational and communication costs. These representations are incorporated into the diffusion model through a pre-trained image-conditioning adapter, thereby providing explicit visual guidance for domain generation. To further improve the quality of synthetic data, FedDoG adapts the strength of visual conditioning according to domain concentration and filters generated samples based on class consistency and predictive entropy. Experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that FedDoG generates higher-quality synthetic images and outperforms existing OSFL baselines in classification accuracy. Meanwhile, FedDoG reduces client upload and computation time by 99.9% and 91.5%, respectively. The code is available at https://github.com/YXTong17/FedDoG.

URLs: https://github.com/YXTong17/FedDoG.

cross Facet-SLAM: Real-Time Triangle Splatting SLAM

Authors: Edward Beng Wai Tan, Siew-Kei Lam

Abstract: Dense SLAM systems based on 3D Gaussian splatting build maps that render photorealistically, but their surface geometry is poor, and obtaining a mesh requires extraction or fusion stages. Triangle splatting avoids this with surface primitives whose vertices can be triangulated directly into a connected mesh, yet SLAM with this representation has so far relied on photometric tracking, which is too slow for localization at sensor rate. We present Facet-SLAM, the first real-time triangle splatting SLAM system for RGB-D cameras. Fast geometric registration is the natural alternative, but it is not sufficient alone, since the mapper's optimization displaces triangles from the observed surface and makes them unreliable registration targets. We therefore separate the two roles: the tracker sets each triangle's centroid from fused depth and registers frames to these centroids by GICP, while the mapper is restricted to non-translating updates via a change of variables. Our system achieves competitive tracking accuracy at around 100 FPS on a desktop GPU and runs at sensor rate on a Jetson AGX Orin. Compared with the photometric approach, it is over 20 times faster, matches rendering quality, and improves surface accuracy, reducing the chamfer distance of the Delaunay mesh on Replica from 1.55 to 0.86 cm. We will release the implementation at https://github.com/edward62740/Facet-SLAM.

URLs: https://github.com/edward62740/Facet-SLAM.

cross WAM-Policy: World Action Models with On-Policy Distillation for Quadrupedal Loco-Manipulation

Authors: Junlin Long, Xu Deng, Yiran Wang, Yue Yang, Yang Zhao, Zeyu Zhang

Abstract: Arm-free quadrupeds can perform loco-manipulation, walking on three legs while manipulating with a front leg, yet no existing policy for it follows language instructions. World action models (WAMs) have emerged as effective language-conditioned manipulation policies, but are pretrained for robot arms and sample slowly. WAM-Policy turns one WAM into a quadrupedal loco-manipulation policy via two stages. The first, Cross-Morphology Policy Adaptation and Acceleration, adapts the arm-centric WAM to quadrupeds and distills the transferred policy into a student with one video and two action steps by on-policy self-distillation (OPSD). This student, however, is bounded by its teacher, which only imitates demonstrations. The second stage, Task-Specific Improvement and Merging, therefore tunes the teacher by reinforcement learning (RL) and merges the slow specialists into one few-step student by multi-teacher on-policy distillation (M-OPD). On QLM-Bench, ten simulated tasks with 1,000 demonstrations synthesized without teleoperation, the transferred policy reaches 77.0%, and the OPSD student keeps 76.7% while sampling 14 times faster. Task-specific RL and M-OPD lift it to 85.0%. We also deploy the OPSD student on a real Unitree Go2 Air. WAM-Policy thus achieves a stronger quadrupedal policy at a fraction of the inference cost.

cross SemPhys: Semantic-Physical Co-Learning for Humanoid Motion Generation via Differentiable World Models

Authors: Zejian Kang, Yeqiang Wang, Zekai Chen, Boyang Shen, Mingyu Ma, Bingqian Wu, Fan Zhou, Jianjie Zhang, Chentao Shen, Wentao Yang, Rui Qian, Xiangru Huang

Abstract: In human-robot collaboration, natural language lets people direct a humanoid's whole-body movements without programming joint trajectories. A common approach generates a kinematic reference from an instruction and relies on a tracking controller to execute it. However, as the controller maintains balance under contact constraints, the executed motion can depart from the reference in ways that change the action it conveys. Our central insight is that semantic alignment must be evaluated directly on the complete motion the robot physically executes. Building on this insight, we introduce \textbf{SemPhys}, a framework that trains motion generators using semantic feedback from simulated executions. A differentiable world model and a fixed tracking controller predict a short segment of a recorded execution. SemPhys inserts this prediction at its original position, scores the complete trajectory against the instruction, and backpropagates through the predicted segment. A paired anchoring loss stabilizes adaptation by keeping each generated reference close to its counterpart from the initial generator. On BONES-SEED, SemPhys largely preserves instruction retrieval accuracy from reference to execution and achieves the highest retrieval accuracy on executed motions among the evaluated methods.

cross UCLIR: Unsupervised Continual Learning using Independent Representations

Authors: Kamel Aouaidjia, Yanchao Duan, Zihui Zhen, Xin Li, Yongfei Lv, Chongsheng Zhang

Abstract: In continual learning, regularization-based methods aim to mimic neuroplasticity by updating network weights to learn new tasks while constraining changes to parameters that are important for previously learned tasks through penalty terms. However, as the number of tasks increases, preserving knowledge from earlier tasks becomes less effective. This is mainly because updates for new tasks are distributed across the entire network, where parameters are highly interdependent due to the dense connectivity between neurons. Inspired by the Exponential Distance Rule (EDR), which states that the number of neural connections decreases with increasing physical distance, learning is naturally locally correlated while inter-area updates remain less task-dependent. Motivated by this principle, in this paper we demonstrate that applying weight regularization to multiple small and independently learned complementary representations is more effective than regularizing a single large shared representation. By decomposing the input into independently learned components, learning new tasks induces only localized parameter updates within specific representation, which limit interference and improve knowledge retention across tasks. We employ independent autoencoders that learn different parts of the input independently through unsupervised regularization. At inference time, a linear classifier is used for evaluation. The experimental results on a sequence of tasks using 5-Splits MNIST, sequence of 5-datasets, 5-Splits CIFAR-10, 10-Splits Tiny ImageNet shows competitive results with supervised and replay-based methods. Code is available.

cross Ego2X: Contact-aware Transfer from Egocentric Human Data to Any Robot

Authors: Xinke Lyu, Bowen Yang, Zhicheng Wang

Abstract: Embodied models depend on real interaction data, yet collecting it on physical robots is slow and labor-intensive. Recent ego-to-robot pipelines retarget first-person view human videos to robot data via transferring hand and arm motion. They judge fidelity mainly by visual appearance. However, trajectories often fail in terms of physics: fingers hover beside objects, and grippers close on empty space. We argue that how the object moves and the contacts that drive the motion count. Thus, we present Ego2X, an object-centric framework that converts monocular egocentric video into physically executable robot data across dexterous hands and parallel grippers. Ego2X centers on CORE (Contact-centric Object-interaction REpresentation), which records each contact on the object by its surface anchor, as well as relative force, grasp mode, and interaction state. Throughout the pipeline, CORE corrects hand-object drift via object-relative constraints, steers inverse kinematics toward contacts the robot must realize, and preserves held objects during arm erasure and robot compositing. On HOT3D, contact-driven correction reduces in-contact hand depth error by 33.7%, while on EgoDex, contact-driven retargeting realizes 62.9% of annotated contacts, surpassing the official HuRo pipeline (27.9%). In RoboTwin 2.0, post-training X-VLA on Ego2X episodes alone, without simulator demonstrations, raises task success rate from 40.5% zero-shot to 67.0% across 200 paired scenes. We will publicly release our conversion pipeline and CORE-annotated dataset for dexterous hands and parallel grippers.

cross Efficient World-Model Planning via Physics-Guided Search Distillation

Authors: Hongyu Zhang, Xiaoshuai Hao, Heye Huang, Qingwen Meng, Wenjun Shi, Jianao Xu, Xin Hu, Xuanru Jin, Jingyi Zhang, Baicang Guo

Abstract: World models support autonomous driving by evaluating candidate trajectories through predicted future scene evolution. However, accurate future prediction does not ensure road-compliant execution; when all candidates violate road boundaries, re-ranking cannot select a feasible trajectory. We propose Physics-Guided Search Distillation (PGSD), a single-view planning framework that combines future representations and execution feedback for verified beyond-pool repair through three components: (1) Execution-Aware Selection checks ego-vehicle road compliance under simulated execution and selects the highest-scoring feasible trajectory; (2) a Clearance-Aware Search Teacher generates beyond-pool corrections and selects demonstrations with larger minimum road-boundary clearance during execution to buffer imitation errors; and (3) a Future-Conditioned Repair Student predicts one correction from rewarder features and execution feedback for single-rollout verification. The pretrained planner and rewarder remain frozen; no future scenes are generated online. On NAVSIM v1, single-view PGSD achieves 90.97 PDMS, exceeding all compared prior single-view planners and retaining 73.2% of the search teacher's PDMS gain over execution-aware selection. On NAVSIM v2 navhard, it reaches 38.96 EPDMS, improving on WorldDrive by 11.24 points (40.5%). The student replaces up to ten search rollouts with a single verification rollout per repair trigger. HUGSIM Hard examples provide qualitative evidence of zero-shot transfer.

cross Joint Image Denoising and Interpolation via Mixed Graph Filtering

Authors: Gene Cheung, Niruhan Viswarupan, Romisaa Elsaidy

Abstract: Images often require both denoising and interpolation, yet treating the two tasks separately is suboptimal: interpolation introduces correlated noise that complicates subsequent denoising, while denoising may remove image details useful for interpolation. In this paper, we study joint image denoising and interpolation from a mixed graph filtering perspective, where denoising is modeled using undirected graph filters and interpolation using directed graph filters. We first establish that, under mild conditions, any (pseudo-)linear denoiser and interpolator can be interpreted as solution filters of maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation problems regularized by undirected and directed graph smoothness priors, respectively. Guided by these results, we formulate graph-based joint denoising and interpolation problems using both directed and undirected graph structures. We characterize when joint denoising/interpolation reduces to a separable pipeline and when a genuinely coupled optimization is required. For the non-separable case, we recover sparse graph operators from given denoisers and interpolators, yielding an efficient linear-system formulation solvable via conjugate gradient. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed joint framework consistently outperforms separate denoising and interpolation pipelines across multiple denoiser-interpolator combinations.

cross Towards Pose-Robust Few-Shot Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Wenhao Li, Zhongyi Han, Yuhao Wei, Liang Han, Yilong Yin, Liqiang Nie

Abstract: Fine-tuning a vision-language-action (VLA) model on a few dozen demonstrations is the standard way to put a manipulation policy on a robot, yet the resulting policy silently assumes that the camera and the robot base stay exactly where they were during data collection: a bumped tripod or a base re-docked a few centimetres away makes it reach for the wrong place. We show that both failures are one phenomenon, a change of the camera-to-base extrinsic that the policy has memorised, and that the robot carries everything needed to undo it. We introduce WACO, a world-anchored canonicalization framework for VLA models: the arm, placed by forward kinematics at the measured joint angles, serves as a metric calibration target, and the static scene serves as the world anchor that tells a moved camera from a moved robot. From a single RGB-D frame, WACO estimates both shifts without ground-truth extrinsics or extra data, canonicalises the image to the calibration viewpoint and the state, action and start pose to the calibration base frame, and fine-tunes the unmodified policy under the artefacts of its own estimator. We also introduce LIBERO-PoseShift, the first benchmark for RGB VLAs with graded camera, base and compound shifts. Across LIBERO-PoseShift, the LIBERO-Plus camera track and four real-world tasks on a UR5e robot, WACO consistently outperforms the standard few-shot recipe and prior viewpoint-robust methods, raising shifted success by 13.2 points in simulation and by 36.7 points on the real robot, recovering 90% of the simulation gap to a ground-truth oracle and 87% of the real-robot gap, and establishing a new state of the art among methods that use no perturbed training data, with no test-time calibration. Project page: https://peacelwh.github.io/WACO/.

URLs: https://peacelwh.github.io/WACO/.

cross Transferable Named Sparse Features for Morphology-based Gleason Grading

Authors: Argyrios Zafeiriou, Athanasios Kallipolitis, Ilias Maglogiannis

Abstract: Deep learning systems grade prostate biopsies at the level of pathologists, but most report a grade without the glandular architecture that justifies it. This work presents a grading system built on a sparse code over the tokens of a pathology foundation model. A sparse autoencoder pretrained on biopsies is aligned with multi-reader annotations, so that 69 of its 2048 features are named after Gleason morphologies such as cribriform glands, cords, and comedonecrosis. The same code feeds a grading network, which reads all features to predict Gleason patterns and the Grade Group, while morphology maps read directly from the named features. We evaluate the system on five public datasets that include needle biopsies, tissue-microarray cores, multi-reader annotations, and three scanners. The morphology maps remain stable across alignment runs and, without retraining, detect cribriform glands in external biopsies (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve, AUROC, 0.849) and pattern 5 in unseen cores when evaluated by up to six pathologists (AUROC 0.846 to 0.919). In an independent biopsy cohort, Grade Group agreement with three pathologists on Leica scans is close to their agreement with each other. Removing the named features reduces the corresponding Gleason pattern predictions by 49% to 98%, with effects larger than those from 20 activity-matched random feature removals. These results show that a shared sparse representation can support competitive Gleason grading while providing named morphological evidence that transfers across datasets and whose contribution to grading can be measured directly.

cross Resolution as Attention for Efficient Agent Memory Perception

Authors: Siqi Xiang, Zhipeng Xu, Shengrui Liu, Zulong Chen, Chunyan Miao, Shijian Lu

Abstract: LLM-based agents accumulate extensive interaction histories when solving complex, long-horizon tasks, making efficient memory management critical to their performance and scalability. While existing memory management methods primarily focus on what to retain or retrieve, they often overlook how much perceptual detail should be allocated to individual past interactions. We posit that effective agent memory depends not only on the information preserved, but also on the granularity at which each past interaction is perceived. Motivated by this insight, we propose Resolution as Attention (RAA), a memory perception mechanism that uses visual resolution as a means of attention allocation across interaction histories. RAA renders past interactions as images and dynamically adjusts their resolution at every step according to their relevance to the agent's upcoming decision, preserving fine-grained information for critical interactions while reducing visual-token consumption for less relevant ones. To adaptively determine the perceptual fidelity of each interaction, we train a lightweight relevance estimator with teacher-model guidance and further optimize it to preserve the agent's decision consistency at a reduced visual-token cost. Experiments across multiple tasks and backbones show that RAA reduces token consumption by over 50% without compromising task performance. Moreover, RAA can be integrated as a plug-in perception layer with existing memory management methods, yielding further token savings and revealing adaptive perception as a complementary dimension of agent memory.

cross Probing Physics in Motion Diffusion Models Against Their Own Estimate

Authors: Saeed Ghorbani

Abstract: Recent studies report that video diffusion models "know more than they show": physical properties decode better from their activations than from their inputs or outputs. But a diffusion model computes its clean-sample estimate from those same activations, so such a comparison measures how easily a readout extracts the information. Whether the model hides anything is a separate question. We set out to find hidden physical knowledge in kinematic text-to-motion diffusion models, where the physics of every intermediate estimate can be computed exactly, and recorded every layer along the sampling trajectories of MDM (two checkpoints) and MotionDiffuse. Against the usual baselines the layers do look ahead of the model's own estimate on balance and flight. With every readout tuned the same way, the estimate closes the gap: for balance and flight, adding a layer gains at most 0.01, a size that a positive control shows we would detect. The fall rate of jumps is the one clear exception. Early in denoising, MDM's layers add about 0.08 in $R^2$ (up to 0.11 in a refit) that the estimate does not yet show, and the gain fades as sampling proceeds; on MotionDiffuse it is small and not robust. The estimate also flags floating samples without training, but aborting them saves under a tenth of the compute. In a video model, plausibility that linear probes miss in Wan 2.1's clean latent becomes readable after one fixed nonlinearity.

cross RECLAIM: Robot-Vision-Informed Adversarial Patch Defense via Embodied Attention Feedback

Authors: Jiayou Zhu, Peng Guo, Litian Zhang, Xinze Shi, Yuting Zhang, Zelong Yu, Yize Tian, Jirong Liu, Zifeng Kang

Abstract: Vision-language-action policies control embodied agents through a perception-action loop grounded in physical interaction, where adversarial patches can corrupt action predictions and thereby alter subsequent observations. To mitigate these threats, many existing defenses rely on policy adaptation or auxiliary models, but require additional training or inference. We introduce RECLAIM, a training-free, inference-time defense that uses a frozen VLA's own attention response to local visual interventions to guide purification during closed-loop embodied manipulation. Our key insight is that attention changes induced by local edits provide feedback for choosing where and how to purify as the robot interacts with its environment. RECLAIM combines this feedback with robot geometry to refine patch localization and select local treatments, then revalidates them as the agent's embodied actions change the observed scene. We evaluate RECLAIM against patches generated by four adversarial attack methods across multiple manipulation task suites under frozen VLA control. Under adversarial patch attacks, RECLAIM succeeds in at least one rollout on 95% of the evaluated tasks and raises rollout success on LIBERO-Object from 0% without defense to 70.0%. Its mean decision time is only 548.9 ms per control step. These results highlight policy-guided visual purification as an effective approach to defending embodied agents against adversarial patches and recovering closed-loop task execution.

cross Hypnos: A Dual-Phase End-to-End Platform for Scalable Continual Learning at the Edge

Authors: Guilherme Guedes, Marco Apolinario Lainez, Charlotte Frenkel

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) enables neural networks to adapt to non-stationary data streams by mitigating catastrophic forgetting, offering a path to robust, private, and adaptable edge devices. Yet, porting CL to real-world edge devices remains challenging for two reasons: (i) online operation, where sensor data arrives one sample at a time and the device must adapt on-the-fly, and (ii) the low footprint of edge devices, which makes traditional error backpropagation training prohibitive. Inspired by the brain's wake and sleep cycles, the former for rapid adaptation with minimal resources and the latter for long-term consolidation, we introduce Hypnos, an open-source dual-phase platform for efficient CL at the edge. In the wake phase (e.g., daily operation), Hypnos relies on a low-footprint system-on-chip with a RISC-V microcontroller unit (MCU) targeting efficient inference and supporting lightweight gradient-free prototype updates. In the sleep phase (e.g., night-time charging), where wall-plug energy and larger memory budgets are available, a smart charger performs rehearsal-based gradient updates for consolidation. Deployed on a ZCU104 field-programmable gate array (FPGA) and synthesized in a 28-nm process, Hypnos is benchmarked on the CORe50 NICv2-391 protocol. It sets a new state-of-the-art accuracy of 78.6%-81.6% for edge CL systems, with an on-device latency of 7.79s-10.54s per sample depending on activation precision, and requires $3.8\times$ fewer learning steps and $22\times$ less learning compute than quantized latent replay at higher accuracy and comparable latency.

cross Piccolo: Co-Evolving Decision Models and Data through Recursive Self-Improvement

Authors: Zhenqing Ling, Qirui Jiao, Daoyuan Chen, Cheng Chen, Xinji Mai, Haowen Gao, Zhuo Zhang, Xuchen Pan, Mengsu Ding, Jiaji Deng, Yuexiang Xie, Xiaoming Qin, Weirui Kuang, Zhaoyang Liu, Yaliang Li, Bolin Ding, Jingren Zhou

Abstract: We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.

cross 4D-TEMPO: Differentiable 4D-STEM ptychography via fast Physics Optimizers

Authors: Ramani Duraiswami, Yichao Zhang

Abstract: Electron ptychography recovers atomic structure from 4D-STEM data: diffraction patterns at multiple positions of a scanned probe. Current codes reconstruct an image and read atoms off it, or fit atoms under an $L_1$ loss; neither gives per-atom uncertainty. We propose 4D-TEMPO, a differentiable physical renderer that fits the atoms, the microscope, and the detector directly to the recorded electron counts under a Poisson likelihood. The output is a list of atoms, each a Gaussian primitive with a 3D position, a thermal width, and a model-conditional error bar from the Fisher information. The Fisher information also conditions the solver, gives the dose (electrons per {\AA}$^2$) each parameter needs, and separates calibration from imaging. On a simulated WSe$_2$ bilayer, starting from the published reconstruction, fitted atoms cut in-plane error by a third (3.2 vs. 4.8 pm) and depth error by 40-61% against its 3D peaks. Depth comes from multiple scattering into the dark field, the faint counts outside the direct beam. The two layers, $10\times$ closer than the depth of field, separate; per-atom depth then follows the layer geometry. When the model is well specified, the bars are calibrated (69% of position coordinates within $1\sigma$). On experimental WSe$_2$, fitted atoms predict held-out patterns better than the three published codes we test. With free probe modes where atoms are incomplete, the same likelihood predicts best on all three held-out benchmarks, matches every WSe$_2$ baseline in 5 min against up to 20.5 h, and passes torchslice at its released setting 180$\times$ sooner on PrScO$_3$. Per-atom error bars, planned doses, and fast reconstruction point to low-dose, real-time 3D imaging of beam-sensitive and dynamic materials.

cross Counterfactual World Models: JEPAs That Reason About "What If"

Authors: Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun, Kerda Varaku

Abstract: World models operate in a world they cannot fully observe. When a hidden disturbance, such as a current pushing a boat, changes the outcome of an action, a counterfactual question follows: what would have happened under a different action, subject to the same disturbance? Its answer separates bad decisions from bad luck. We prove that world models trained on factual transitions cannot answer it on their own, whatever their data or capacity. For many physical systems, the observed outcome and a simple property of the dynamics suffice: natura non facit saltus, nature makes no leaps. When actions and disturbances act as separate forces, their interaction is second-order in time, so the counterfactual outcome is, to first order, the observed outcome plus the difference between the world model's predictions under the alternative and factual actions. This yields a counterfactual world model with no counterfactual data, extra parameters, or retraining. For transitions of duration $h$, we prove an $O(h^2)$ bound on the counterfactual error relative to the disturbance variance, establish a matching minimax lower bound from unpaired factual data, and characterize failures at discontinuities, coordinate folds, and disturbances that scale the command. We validate these predictions from pixels with JEPA world models on frozen DINOv2 and masked autoencoder (MAE) encoders. On four rendered simulators, our approach improves counterfactual $R^2$ from $38$--$46\%$ to $86$--$96\%$ with the same world models; on a fifth, an underwater vehicle, it reaches $90$--$94\%$ with sixteen times more factual data. Four further frozen encoders, DINOv3, SigLIP~2, CLIP and EVA-02, give $81$--$95\%$ on the same simulators. In hindsight planning, it finds the action that would have reached the goal in $35$--$69\%$ of events, against $14$--$23\%$ for an oracle planner that knows the simulator but not the outcome.

cross Synthesizing hyperspectral Data using generative Models to train spectral Unmixing Methods for low-cost Crop Residue Cover Mapping

Authors: Ege Artan, Shuo Chen, Andrew Peng, Sammuel Aldrich Karya, Kyaw Thiha

Abstract: The spectral range of the Field Imaging Nanosatellite for Crop residue Hyperspectral Mapping (FINCH), developed by the University of Toronto Aerospace Team's Space System Division, poses significant challenges for hyperspectral unmixing to determine crop residue cover fractional abundances. The severe lack of standard indices necessitates the use of complex, data-driven unmixing models. Complex unmixing models require dense, well-distributed manifolds, whereas ground-truth datasets are sparse and expensive. To better leverage the information content in existing ground-truth datasets, this study presents a framework that decouples interpolation and abundance-mapping estimation via an intermediary conditional data generator, contrasting with traditional single-model unmixing pipelines. This decoupling enables the inclusion of physical prior knowledge about spectra, which is not natively accessible to unmixing models, thereby artificially expanding the dataset to serve as a Monte Carlo approximation of the population risk. To test this hypothesis, we have proposed two generator models: a 1D Conditional U-Net with Conformer Layers (GD-Streamline) and a Dual-Path Transformer Conditional Variational AutoEncoder (TCVAE), and two unmixing models: Multi-Layered Perceptrons (MLP) and Fourier Neural Operators (FNO). The results of the study indicate a need for rigorous integration and introduction of spectral priors within the scope of the proposed decoupling framework to prevent domain and mode collapse and hallucination by the generator models; otherwise, this leads to falsely approximated data manifolds, ultimately resulting in unsatisfactory and out-of-distribution unmixing performance.

cross $Z$-sight: Unifying Vision-Language-Action and Latent World Modeling

Authors: Junjie Ye, Yiqi Zhao, Muchen Xu, Celina Shiyu Wang, Hao Dong, Ruohai Ge, Rong Xue, Benjamin J Kolligs, Aditya Dutt, Sasanka Nagavalli, Nassim A. Samad, Gaurav S. Sukhatme, Jyotirmoy V. Deshmukh, Yue Wang, Yuliang Guo

Abstract: We introduce $Z$-sight, a unified robot foundation model that couples vision-language-action (VLA) and generative latent world modeling. Video-based world action models (WAMs) show the value of joint future-and-action generation for control, yet inherit the heavy cost of video generators. Recent latent-space policies avoid pixel generation, yet typically align intermediate features or rely on frozen target encoders, decoupling future prediction from the VLM's native feature space. $Z$-sight instead predicts the future directly in the visual token space in which its VLM perceives the present. Coupling a foresight expert and a continuous action expert with the VLM in a mixture-of-transformers, $Z$-sight jointly denoises future spatial tokens and actions at inference without pixel reconstruction or external encoders. Trained on human video and robot trajectories, $Z$-sight achieves 81.9% success on SIMPLE (+9.6 points over the strongest baseline) and 73.3% across six Unitree G1 tasks (+20.0 points) at 110 ms per chunk, while remaining competitive on tabletop benchmarks.

replace Coarse-to-Fine Sparse Transformer for Hyperspectral Image Reconstruction

Authors: Yuanhao Cai, Jing Lin, Xiaowan Hu, Haoqian Wang, Xin Yuan, Yulun Zhang, Radu Timofte, Luc Van Gool

Abstract: Many algorithms have been developed to solve the inverse problem of coded aperture snapshot spectral imaging (CASSI), i.e., recovering the 3D hyperspectral images (HSIs) from a 2D compressive measurement. In recent years, learning-based methods have demonstrated promising performance and dominated the mainstream research direction. However, existing CNN-based methods show limitations in capturing long-range dependencies and non-local self-similarity. Previous Transformer-based methods densely sample tokens, some of which are uninformative, and calculate the multi-head self-attention (MSA) between some tokens that are unrelated in content. This does not fit the spatially sparse nature of HSI signals and limits the model scalability. In this paper, we propose a novel Transformer-based method, coarse-to-fine sparse Transformer (CST), firstly embedding HSI sparsity into deep learning for HSI reconstruction. In particular, CST uses our proposed spectra-aware screening mechanism (SASM) for coarse patch selecting. Then the selected patches are fed into our customized spectra-aggregation hashing multi-head self-attention (SAH-MSA) for fine pixel clustering and self-similarity capturing. Comprehensive experiments show that our CST significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods while requiring cheaper computational costs. The code and models will be released at https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/MST . An online Gradio demo is available at https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/MST

URLs: https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/MST, https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/MST

replace MST++: Multi-stage Spectral-wise Transformer for Efficient Spectral Reconstruction

Authors: Yuanhao Cai, Jing Lin, Zudi Lin, Haoqian Wang, Yulun Zhang, Hanspeter Pfister, Radu Timofte, Luc Van Gool

Abstract: Existing leading methods for spectral reconstruction (SR) focus on designing deeper or wider convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to learn the end-to-end mapping from the RGB image to its hyperspectral image (HSI). These CNN-based methods achieve impressive restoration performance while showing limitations in capturing the long-range dependencies and self-similarity prior. To cope with this problem, we propose a novel Transformer-based method, Multi-stage Spectral-wise Transformer (MST++), for efficient spectral reconstruction. In particular, we employ Spectral-wise Multi-head Self-attention (S-MSA) that is based on the HSI spatially sparse while spectrally self-similar nature to compose the basic unit, Spectral-wise Attention Block (SAB). Then SABs build up Single-stage Spectral-wise Transformer (SST) that exploits a U-shaped structure to extract multi-resolution contextual information. Finally, our MST++, cascaded by several SSTs, progressively improves the reconstruction quality from coarse to fine. Comprehensive experiments show that our MST++ significantly outperforms other state-of-the-art methods. In the NTIRE 2022 Spectral Reconstruction Challenge, our approach won the First place. Code and pre-trained models are publicly available at https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/MST-plus-plus . An online Gradio demo is available on HuggingFace at https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/MST-plus-plus
