replace Localize, Don't Beautify: Client-Side Control of Image-Editing APIs for Cosmetic Surgery Previews

Authors: Sukhrobbek Ilyosbekov

Abstract: Ask a commercial image editor to preview a cosmetic procedure and it will often change more of the face than the request names: a jawline edit can also smooth the forehead or alter the eyes. Existing methods for confining an edit to one region require access to the model's internals, which a public editing API does not expose. We ask how much control is possible from the client side alone. In a pilot benchmark, five current commercial editing configurations perform facelift-style jaw-neck and rhinoplasty edits on synthetic faces under two levels of client-side control: the prompt alone, and a masked composite that cuts the edited region out of the response and pastes it back onto the original photograph through a landmark-derived mask. Of 162 attempted edits, 151 could be scored. ArcFace cosine measures identity preservation; a CIELAB pixel-change ratio measures how much change lands inside the requested region rather than a protected facial zone. On 15 faces, the masked composite improved localization over the paired prompt-only output by a median of 0.315 (95% face-clustered bootstrap interval 0.279-0.366) while retaining most of the target-region change. Editors differed in edit strength and identity retention, and one returned near-copies for the facelift request. This is a study of control, not clinical accuracy: the faces are synthetic, no surgeons rated the outputs, and each condition was generated once. Within that scope, keeping a surgical preview inside its intended region needs no access to the model; a mask and composite on the client enforce it across every editor tested, at low provider cost.

replace OmniEdit-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Instruction-based Video Editing

Authors: Chenxuan Miao, Yutong Feng, Yi Lu, Yunfeng Yan, Donglian Qi, Shiwei Zhang, Yu Liu, Xi Chen, Hengshuang Zhao

Abstract: Instruction-based video editing (IVE) offers diverse applications, yet evaluating editing models remains challenging. Existing benchmarks suffer from two key limitations: narrow task coverage inherited from image editing that overlooks video-specific dimensions, and metrics that inadequately capture instruction fidelity, allowing visually plausible but incorrect edits to receive high scores. To address these limitations, we introduce OmniEdit-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark covering spatial, temporal, audio, and reference-based editing. It distinguishes between explicit and implicit instructions and incorporates reasoning-based scenarios to reflect real-world editing requirements. We further propose an evaluation framework that assesses accuracy, preservation, realism, and consistency using both human judges and a state-of-the-art vision-language model (VLM). An accuracy-aware penalty mechanism conditions the other three scores on accuracy, prioritizing instruction fidelity and penalizing plausible but incorrect edits. Experiments on prominent open-source and commercial IVE models reveal substantial room for improvement. OmniEdit-Bench provides a structured testbed for identifying model limitations and advancing instruction-based video editing. Project page: https://omniedit-bench.github.io.

URLs: https://omniedit-bench.github.io.

replace TEA: Text Encoder Alignment for Robust Concept Erasure in Text-to-Image Models

Authors: Alireza Dehghanpour Farashah, Zhuan Shi, Negar Rostamzadeh, Golnoosh Farnadi

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models can be misused to generate harmful content through adversarial or paraphrased prompts that bypass built-in safety mechanisms. Existing concept erasure methods often suffer from limited robustness against adversarial prompts, degradation of benign generation quality, or reliance on inference-time interventions that introduce persistent computational overhead. To address these limitations, we formulate concept erasure as a domain alignment problem in the text representation space. We propose a lightweight Text Encoder Alignment framework (TEA) that fine-tunes only the text encoder while keeping the generative backbone fully frozen. Given concept-anchor prompt pairs, our method trains a discriminator to distinguish token-level representations of concept-bearing prompts from those of safe anchor prompts, while updating the text encoder to make these representations indistinguishable. TEA introduces zero inference-time overhead and trains in seconds with only a few fine-tuning steps, making it practical for large-scale deployment. Despite this efficiency, TEA achieves state-of-the-art erasure robustness against black-box and white-box adversarial attacks on Stable Diffusion v1.4, while preserving generation quality on benign prompts. Furthermore, TEA is model-agnostic and achieves the lowest attack success rate on Stable Diffusion v3.5, extending concept erasure to Rectified Flow Transformer architectures with T5 conditioning, which remain largely unexplored by prior methods. Code is available at https://github.com/alirezafarashah/TEA

URLs: https://github.com/alirezafarashah/TEA

replace MVC-Bench: Benchmarking Calibration of Medical Vision-Language Models

Authors: Ashshak Sharifdeen, Shihab Aaqil Ahamed, Ufaq Khan, Muhammad Akhtar Munir, Sujair Ibrahim, Mohamed Rafeek Mareer Ahamed, Yutong Xie, Imran Razzak, Muhammad Haris Khan

Abstract: Reliable evaluation of vision-language models (VLMs) and medical vision-language models (Medical-VLMs) requires calibrated confidence, particularly under realistic clinical conditions. However, existing efforts mainly focused on improving accuracy, leaving calibration in the medical domain underexplored. To this end, we propose MVC-Bench, a calibration-centric benchmark for medical image classification with VLMs and Medical-VLMs. MVC-Bench assesses the calibration across three axes: (i) robustness to modality, backbone, and domain shift (ii) effectiveness of calibration strategies and prompt-tuning methods (iii) stability under prompt-template and random-seed variations. The benchmark covers eight different backbones, three medical modalities, including fundus imaging, histopathology, and chest X-ray under in-domain and domain shift settings. It compares post-hoc calibration, train-time calibration, and zero-shot inference methods, together with six prompt-tuning methods. Across more than 1638 controlled experiments, we report accuracy and Expected Calibration Error (ECE) as primary metrics, and further report results with complementary calibration measures, including Maximum Calibration Error (MCE) and Adaptive Calibration Error (ACE). We further investigate the underlying causes of miscalibration in VLMs and Medical-VLMs and propose a simple train-time calibration method, Multi-Class Margin (MCM) regularization, which achieves lowest ECE on 10 out of 12 settings in in-domain and remains competitive under domain shifts. Collectively, MVC-Bench provides a structured evaluation framework and actionable guidance for improving calibration in safety-critical medical workflows.

replace Video-OPSD: Exploiting Privileged Visual Evidence for On-Policy Self-Distillation in Video Large Language Models

Authors: Ziyue Wang, Shiqi Huang, Weiwen Xu, Bihan Wen, Xudong Jiang

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) has recently emerged as an effective post-training paradigm that provides dense token-level supervision from a privileged self-teacher, yet it remains underexplored for Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs). Existing methods typically construct privileged teachers by augmenting their context with additional information while keeping the primary input unchanged for both teacher and student. In video reasoning, however, privileged information can also be found within the primary input itself: videos contain substantial temporal redundancy, and only a few frames carry the evidence needed to answer a question. Building on this observation, we present $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$, an OPSD framework that exploits privileged visual evidence for both self-teacher construction and knowledge transfer. First, our Evidence-Grounded Self-Teacher conditions the teacher exclusively on annotated evidence frames, while the student reasons over a broader set of frames that contains them. This focused visual context enables more targeted supervision without introducing visual content unavailable to the student. Second, our Evidence-Guided Token Optimization adaptively weights token-level distillation according to an estimate of each reasoning token's reliance on privileged visual evidence, thereby emphasizing perceptually grounded reasoning. Across five video benchmarks and three Qwen-VL backbones, $\textbf{Video-OPSD}$ consistently outperforms Standard OPSD by 1.8-2.3 percentage points on average, establishing an effective and efficient post-training approach for Video-LLMs.

replace Visual Token Coding for Video Multimodal Large Language Models

Authors: Chenxin Fang, Tao Chen, JunChao You, Jun Peng, Yiyi Zhou, Rongrong Ji

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a new token compression paradigm for video Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), termed Visual Token Coding ($VTC$). Inspired by classical video coding principles, e.g., HEVC, VTC performs structured compression by predicting the I/P frames of a video and measuring their frame-wise residuals to estimate token redundancy. Based on this base paradigm, we further extend VTC with a set of dynamic budgeting designs towards more cost-effective token coding, termed $VTC_{dy}$. To validate VTC and $VTC_{dy}$, we apply them to three MLLMs and conduct experiments on a set of video understanding benchmarks. The experimental results show that as a base paradigm, VTC can achieve an average performance retention of 98.4% on Qwen3-VL under a 50% token compression ratio, and the enhanced $VTC_{dy}$ can further improve the retention to 100.3%, outperforming a set of compared methods. Moreover, as a plug-and-play design, VTC requires no additional tuning of MLLMs for token coding. Our code is given in the supplementary materials.

replace RadMatch: Auditable Radiology Report Evaluation via Finding-Level Matching

Authors: Charles Corbi\`ere, L\'eo Machado, Baptiste Callard, Aubin Charley, Korentin Le Floch, Martin J. Willemink, Pierre Manceron, Corentin Dancette

Abstract: As AI systems are increasingly used to draft radiology reports, reliably evaluating their clinical quality remains a critical challenge. We introduce RadMatch, a multi-stage LLM-based framework that turns report evaluation into a structured, auditable finding-level record. It extracts findings from a reference and a candidate report, matches them by clinical equivalence, grades each match over ten clinical attributes, and gives every match and unmatched finding a verdict and a clinical-significance tier. To validate metrics on CT, we build and release MetricEval-BodyCT, the first public metric-evaluation benchmark of chest and abdomen CT reports annotated by radiologists error by error. On it and three chest X-ray benchmarks, RadMatch with an open 31B judge on consumer GPUs agrees with radiologists more closely than previous published metrics, and even reaches inter-expert agreement on ReXVal and on the significant errors of MetricEval-BodyCT. Radiologists auditing its decisions on 60 studies confirm 98.5% of its matches and 93.6% of the errors it counts. When used to evaluate state-of-the-art radiology report generation models, RadMatch shows that most CT models, unlike several chest X-ray models, actually leave more significant errors than an image-blind normal template, as their added unsupported findings outnumber the reference findings they correctly report. We provide RadMatch's code, its dashboard, the MetricEval-BodyCT dataset and all per-report scores.

replace Adapting a Foundation Model for Lunar Surface Height Estimation

Authors: Patrick Bauer, Marius Schwinning, Melanie Siegel, Andreas Weinmann, Hichem Snoussi

Abstract: Digital elevation models (DEMs) can provide accurate height information, making it invaluable for analyzing the lunar surface. Due to recent advancements in computer vision, the focus is on generating DEMs using monocular depth estimation via deep learning. Classic lunar crater detection methods rely mainly on 2D image data. Our goal is to address this issue by developing a relative lunar surface height estimator that could provide additional information for crater localization. In this letter, we present a methodology that builds on the zero-shot relative depth estimation model Depth Anything V2 (DAV2). Other works have been using it as a state-of-the-art comparison for their proposed lunar DEM estimation method, but without adaptations to the target domain. Thus, it may underperform. Therefore, we propose a fine-tuning strategy with publicly available SPG-derived DEM data of the lunar surface, and refer to it as DEM-DAV2. Specifically, we introduce new learnable parameters in the Transformer-based encoder with low-rank adaptation (LoRA), and we unfreeze certain layers in the model's decoder. On our test set, the proposed DEM-DAV2 reduces the average MAE and RMSE by $\sim$80\% and $\sim$79\%, respectively, compared to the zero-shot DAV2. This demonstrates a significant improvement in performance compared to the zero-shot model, effectively transforming DAV2 into a reliable relative height estimator of the lunar surface. However, the predictions remain relative and require scale recovery from a low-resolution DEM such as SLDEM.

replace OracleZoom: Reference-Constrained Recursive Image Super-Resolution Beyond Ground Truth

Authors: Shubhashis Roy Dipta, Nobin Sarwar, Md Rizwan Parvez

Abstract: Recursive image Super-Resolution (SR) reaches extreme magnification by feeding each prediction back into the same model, like zooming into an image again and again. Real ground truth for deep zooms is impractical to capture (a full $256\times$ target would be a 52 GB image), so predictions at deeper zooms have no supervision. Yet the last ground-truth image still contains the region that every deeper zoom magnifies. OracleZoom turns this region into a training signal. Inspired by on-policy self-distillation, the model trains on its own prediction for one recursive step ($4\times \rightarrow 16\times$), and a cross-scale loss projects each $16\times$ prediction back to the last ground truth at $4\times$. This projection can verify only coarse content, so a no-reference quality objective guides the remaining fine detail, and a latent prior stops this objective from adding hallucinated artifacts. On seven datasets, OracleZoom achieves the highest CLIPIQA at every zoom depth (mean 0.713 vs. 0.621 for Chain-of-Zoom), with the largest gains at the deepest zooms, even though training stops at $16\times$ and optimizes a different quality metric. Pooled over two ground-truth-anchored judges, this added detail comes at the same hallucination rate as Chain-of-Zoom at $16\times$ and a lower rate at $4\times$, and removing the cross-scale loss raises hallucination at $16\times$.

replace Synthetic Leprosy Image Generation Using Mask-Conditioned Latent Diffusion and Transfer Learning from Large Chronic Wound Datasets

Authors: Yusuf Abdulkadir

Abstract: Machine learning for neglected tropical diseases is limited by data, not algorithms: public annotated image sets for leprosy (Hansen's disease) number in the hundreds. We ask whether a model trained on abundant chronic wound photography transfers to this low-data regime. We build a three-stage pipeline: a DeepLabV3-ResNet50 segmenter supplies lesion masks for two wound datasets that ship without them; a mask-conditioned latent diffusion model built from Stable Diffusion 1.5 components is trained on 3,280 wound crops; and the model is fine-tuned on leprosy image-mask pairs from CO2Wounds-V2 (764 images of 96 patients). We evaluate on 136 held-out leprosy lesions using LPIPS anchored by a real-versus-real baseline, kernel inception distance (KID), and a paired design that runs both models on the same input. Fine-tuning moved the generated distribution toward real held-out leprosy images: KID fell from 0.167 to 0.089, and LPIPS distance to real images from 0.806 to 0.765, with non-overlapping 95% confidence intervals. Giving the base model the leprosy prompt alone did not reproduce the gain (KID 0.250). For a fixed input, fine-tuning changes the output less than a new random seed does (paired LPIPS 0.544 versus 0.728): it keeps layout and alters tissue color and texture. The generated set shows no mode collapse (internal diversity 0.734 versus 0.667 for real images). A dataset audit shows that a majority of training images carry synthetic flat regions from preprocessing, which the fine-tuned model partly reproduces. Chronic wound photography is a viable donor domain for leprosy lesion synthesis; the remaining barrier is semantic control rather than image quality.

replace Small Object Detection in Drone Aerial Imagery with LAF-YOLOv10

Authors: Quratulain Nayeem, Fahmina Taranum, Mohammed Mudassir Uddin, Sara Rizwan, Samia Areej

Abstract: General-purpose object detectors lose accuracy on UAV footage, where targets span only a handful of pixels and onboard compute is limited. Prior work composes independently-validated architectural techniques into one detector, assuming gains reported in isolation transfer once combined. We stress-test that assumption directly. LAF-YOLOv10 integrates four techniques into YOLOv10n: a Partial Convolution C2f (PC-C2f) backbone block, an Attention-Guided Feature Pyramid Network (AG-FPN), a P2 detection head replacing the large-object P5 head, and Wise-IoU v3 regression, asking whether their combined effect matches what each contributes alone. We train LAF-YOLOv10 three times (seeds 42, 123, 256) on VisDrone-DET2019, benchmark against unmodified YOLOv10n, and use TIDE error decomposition, per-category breakdown, per-component ablation, attention/loss comparisons, zero-shot transfer to UAVDT, and held-out/test-dev evaluation to localize where the combination succeeds or fails. Composability does not hold here. LAF-YOLOv10 reaches 24.0+/-0.4% mAP@0.5 at 2.14M parameters, 7.8 points below YOLOv10n (31.8%), a deficit that transfers to UAVDT (-10.0 points) and is confirmed by held-out and test-dev evaluation (23.5%, 22.5%). Background false positives, localization error, and duplicate detections move in the direction AG-FPN and Wise-IoU were designed to push. Ablation traces the deficit to a specific source: the P2/-P5 head swap costs 2.5 points independently plus a 2.5-point interaction penalty when layered onto a backbone weakened by PC-C2f, whose own 2.0-point loss is consistent with a partial pretrained-weight transplant (73/150 backbone tensors transfer). The failure is attributable to a specific interaction, not the four components individually. Composability must be verified directly, not assumed. Code/checkpoints: https://github.com/Mudassiruddin7/Small-Object-Detection-in-UAV-Imagery.

URLs: https://github.com/Mudassiruddin7/Small-Object-Detection-in-UAV-Imagery.

replace Ischemic Stroke Segmentation and Net Water Uptake Quantification on Multicenter Non-Contrast CT Using Supervised Target-Domain Adaptation

Authors: Linus Britt, Maximilian Nielsen, Susan Klapproth, Andre Kemmling, Michael H. Lev, Gabriel Broocks, Rene Werner, Thilo Sentker

Abstract: Quantitative assessment of infarct hypodensity on non-contrast computed tomography (NCCT), including net water uptake (NWU), requires manual or semi-manual lesion delineation, often guided by CT perfusion or diffusion-weighted MRI, limiting clinical applicability. Automated NCCT segmentation could enable such biomarker extraction but remains challenging across heterogeneous multicenter data. This study developed and externally tested a deep learning framework for ischemic stroke segmentation and NWU quantification on NCCT. In this retrospective multicenter study of 801 patients from four datasets, an nnU-Net-based model with foundation-model initialization, extended by modules comparing local features with the contralateral hemisphere, was trained on NCCT scans from the University Medical Center Hamburg-Eppendorf and the Acute Ischemic Stroke Dataset. The model was fine-tuned on target-domain subsets of the Boston (n=11) and ISLES (n=75) datasets, with evaluation on held-out cases. Segmentations and NWU were compared with expert references. Internally, mean Dice increased from 0.53 (conventional nnU-Net) to 0.57 (proposed approach). After fine-tuning, the proposed approach achieved median Dice of 0.56 for acute lesion segmentation (Boston) and 0.26 for post-treatment final-infarct prediction (ISLES), where small lesions predominated. For lesions >=30 mL, median Dice increased to 0.67 and 0.57, respectively. On the Boston dataset, automated and reference NWU agreed (concordance correlation coefficient 0.93, mean absolute error 1.24 percentage points). Contralateral feature comparison and target-domain adaptation supported NCCT-only infarct segmentation across heterogeneous external cohorts, although performance was lower for post-treatment final-infarct prediction. The approach enabled low-error NWU quantification from baseline NCCT without advanced imaging or manual annotation.

replace Distilling a Geospatial Foundation Model into Compact Surface-Water Segmenters

Authors: Fabian Schmalstieg, Karsten Mueller, Wojciech Samek

Abstract: Geospatial foundation models segment surface water accurately, but their size limits deployment on edge hardware. Therefore, we distill a 300-million-parameter Prithvi-EO-2.0 teacher, fine-tuned on the 252 hand-labeled Sen1Floods11 training images, into compact student models. The teacher labels 2,500 Sentinel-2 images around flood alerts and supervises the students via its labels and probability maps, without manual annotation. A 0.7-million-parameter EfficientViT-B0 student achieves the teacher's accuracy in water intersection over union (IoU) on WorldFloods-v2, STURM-Flood and 97 held-back WorldFloods events. Unlike prior work, we evaluate every model on four test sets from three public datasets and on two new dry-scene sets. Controlled comparisons show that teacher labels achieve human-level annotation quality for student training on the same images. The teacher's probability maps account for much of its value, and the full pipeline adds 5.7 IoU points on the held-back events over training on hand labels alone. All students, with parameter counts ranging from about 1,000 to 32.5 million, perform within 2 IoU points of the teacher on WorldFloods-v2 and STURM-Flood. On a Jetson Xavier NX, the main student runs as a 1.6-megabyte mixed 8-bit integer and 16-bit floating-point engine at 4.9 milliseconds per 512-by-512 image, and a 12,230-parameter student runs in full 8-bit integer format within 0.5 IoU points of its 32-bit version. replace Rethinking the Reconstruction-Generation Gap in Representation Autoencoders

Authors: Hongyang Du, Yunfei Xie, Junjie Ye, Jiawei Yang, Xiaoyan Cong, Haodong Zhang, Yongchao Huang, Haiyu Wu, Zongxia Li, Shihang Gui, Dawei Liu, Runhao Li, Jingcheng Ni, Chen Wei, Randall Balestriero, Yue Wang

Abstract: Fusing shallower encoder layers into a Representation Autoencoder (RAE) sharpens pixel reconstruction but degrades diffusion generation. Prior studies and the research community commonly attribute this trade-off to the latent content itself, assuming detail-rich features are harder to model. We show that this gap stems from geometric mismatch at the encoder-decoder interface. Freezing a foundation encoder preserves rich visual information, but imposes an encoder-native Euclidean metric shaped without pixel decoding, whereas the decoder amplifies sampling errors sharply along sensitive directions. At fixed information, reducing pixel error admits two dual geometric interventions: regularizing the decoder to flatten its sensitivity to latent variations, and aligning diffusion coordinates with the frozen decoder's metric at identical clean reconstruction. On ImageNet-256, aligning diffusion coordinates largely closes the guided generation penalty of multi-layer fusion and reaches state-of-the-art 0.984 gFID and 2.14 FDr6 in 80 epochs. RAEs should take information from the encoder and geometry from the decoder.

replace Difficulty-Aware Statistical Matching for Dataset Distillation

Authors: Hongxu Ma, Guang Li, Shijie Wang, Dongzhan Zhou, Suorong Yang, Baoli Sun, Takahiro Ogawa, Miki Haseyama, Zhihui Wang

Abstract: Dataset distillation (DD) condenses a large original set into a small distilled set with high training utility. Decoupled statistical matching methods substantially reduce distillation time and memory overhead while achieving strong performance. However, they typically supervise all distilled data with running statistics estimated from the entire original set. These statistics mainly capture the average feature distribution while ignoring the difficulty differences among original data, making them insufficient for characterizing the difficulty structure of the original data. To address this issue, we propose Difficulty-aware Statistical Matching (DSM) for Dataset Distillation. After pretraining, DSM uses the Global Difficulty Score (GDS) to evaluate image difficulty, sorts the original data according to the scores, and then partitions it into IPC (Images per Class of distilled data) difficulty groups. During distillation, Statistics Updated Again (SUA) updates the teacher's BN running statistics using original data from each group via forward passes, providing difficulty-specific supervision for each corresponding distilled batch. Meanwhile, Initial Sample Screening (ISS) initializes distilled data with original images from the corresponding group, providing an effective starting point for precise matching. Experiments across multiple datasets and model architectures demonstrate the effectiveness of DSM, showing that it broadens the difficulty range of distilled samples and further improves the final performance. Code will be released.

replace Reimagine Video Dynamics: A Unified Framework for Video Editing with Editable Spatiotemporal Tokens

Authors: Yu Yuan, Yawen Lu, Guoxian Song, Kevin Duarte, Ratheesh Kalarot, Di Chang, Xijun Wang, Stanley H. Chan

Abstract: Most video editing methods can change how a scene looks but offer limited control over how it moves. We introduce Reimagine Video Dynamics (RVD), a unified framework for editing actions, motion pace, and appearance with reusable dynamics tokens and a shared renderer. The tokens capture motion from the source video and retain an explicit temporal axis. A language-guided editor transforms them to change an action or event. Resampling them changes the pace of motion, while keeping them fixed allows the renderer to reproduce the motion with a new appearance. We train the renderer to reconstruct source videos. We pretrain the editor on motion from real videos, then fine-tune it on counterfactual pairs. Across 450 dynamics edits, RVD achieves the highest instruction-following score and an 80.0\% pass rate. The same renderer supports retiming and motion reuse without additional task-specific training.

replace Masked Swingers: Harnessing Data Augmentation to Advance Autoencoders for Self-Supervised Learning

Authors: Anthony Fuller, Scott C. Lowe, Daniel G. Kyrollos, Graham W. Taylor, Evan Shelhamer, James R. Green

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) trains on inputs alone, allowing us to make models that are more useful in more settings. The autoencoder SSL framework learns by reconstructing its own input after information loss through a bottleneck or noise injection. Masked autoencoders (MAE) are the most successful instantiation of this framework: they encode a random subset of patches, then decode the masked-out patches. In this work, we introduce key modifications to improve MAEs. Our method augments an image in two different ways, then masks and encodes each view separately. It then exchanges the global representations (CLS tokens) between views before decoding the masked patches. By design, our Masked Swingers encourages learning a view-agnostic summary of the image for efficient transfer. We perform extensive experiments, and find Masked Swingers outperforms the official MAE checkpoint by +10.7% averaged over 10 frozen-feature tasks at equal pre-training compute. Gains are highest on fine-grained tasks, e.g., instance retrieval. Swingers also reduces error by 64% relative to MAE on three new state-probing datasets for world modeling, while keeping the fine-tunability of MAE. Post-training by swinging nearest neighbors gains again, e.g., by +5% on IN-1K kNN, opening the door to learning invariances via autoencoding.

replace No Corners Cut: State-Grounded Transitions for Mid-Stream Prompt Switches in Video Generation

Authors: Zejing Rao, Ketong Ren, Xiaoqiang Liu, Yiping Meng, Guoxin Zhang, Fan Tang

Abstract: Streaming video generators allow users to dynamically modulate video synthesis via mid-stream prompt switching. Existing streaming methods can respond to the updated instruction while still cutting corners, prematurely realizing goals or taking heuristic shortcuts that bypass necessary intermediate state changes needed for a plausible transition. In this study, we present SEGUE, a novel framework that makes this process explicit and trains the generator to execute these transitions faithfully. At each switch, a training-free planner parses the latest frame and prompts, writes a few segue prompts with roles and durations, and then hands control back to the user's prompt. Furthermore, to address the inherent difficulty of training causal models on short-lived temporal schedules without corrupting preparatory supervision, we introduce SpanDMD, which evaluates each active prompt using the full rollout as temporal context while retaining its DMD residual only within the prompt's assigned span. On OpenTrans-360, a benchmark of 1,800 switches that scores how the old state exits and the new one begins, SEGUE ranks first on all eight transition metrics and raises the overall score over the strongest baseline from 0.866 to 0.887. It leads on four of six StreamAV-Bench instruction-response metrics and achieves the highest CLIP score in all five post-switch segments of VBench-Long evaluation, while the planner transfers to frozen autoregressive generators without retraining.

replace An Invariant Tangent-Angle Descriptor and a Band U-Net for 2D Fragment Adjacency Prediction

Authors: Guillaume Brouillette (Universit\'e du Qu\'ebec \`a Trois-Rivi\`eres, Trois-Rivi\`eres, Canada), Alain Goupil (Universit\'e du Qu\'ebec \`a Trois-Rivi\`eres, Trois-Rivi\`eres, Canada), Pierre-Olivier Paris\'e (Universit\'e du Qu\'ebec \`a Trois-Rivi\`eres, Trois-Rivi\`eres, Canada), Fadel Tour\'e (Universit\'e du Qu\'ebec \`a Trois-Rivi\`eres, Trois-Rivi\`eres, Canada)

Abstract: Reassembling a broken artefact begins with deciding which fragments are adjacent. This paper addresses adjacency prediction for pairs of 2D fragments by analysing their contours. We propose a two-stage pipeline. In the first stage, each contour point is encoded using a tangent-angle profile, a descriptor that does not depend on rotation, translation or the choice of starting point. Pairs of profiles are then used to build an adjacency matrix, each entry computed either from a log-likelihood ratio or using a small 1D convolutional network. In the second stage, a U-Net classifies this matrix to predict whether the two fragments are adjacent. We compared our pipeline with two approaches from the literature using their own datasets. The results show that our approach outperforms them by reaching a Recall@10 of 0.823 versus 0.564 on the real dataset of PairingNet, and an accuracy of 0.979 versus 0.916 on Beaulac's dataset.

replace Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs

Authors: Tongxi Wu, Yuyang Cheng, Jiacheng Xian, Hu Huang, Zhihao Wu, Menglin Yang, Jian Zhang, Yang Gao

Abstract: Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Most prior attacks rely on specially constructed visual inputs to carry or support harmful intent. In this paper, we uncover a different vulnerability: task-relevant scene semantics alone can shift an MLLM from refusal to compliance, even when the visual input introduces no additional attack instructions and the original task intent remains unchanged. We formalize this failure as a scene-grounded visual safety vulnerability and introduce SceneSafetyBench to investigate it across eight risk categories and nine white- and black-box MLLMs. Our analysis reveals that this failure is associated with how task-relevant visual evidence influences downstream safety representations, suggesting that visual grounding alone cannot fully explain the observed safety shift. Building on this finding, we propose Scene-Grounded Attacks (SGA), which preserve the original task while constructing validated scene-grounded queries and combining their independent responses. Across nine target MLLMs, SGA achieves an average attack success rate (ASR) of 86.17%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 8.55 percentage points and achieving the highest ASR on eight models. Our results identify scene semantics as an overlooked attack surface in MLLMs and highlight a growing tension between stronger visual grounding and robust multimodal safety alignment.

replace CAPTOR: a vision-language foundation model for Chest-Abdomen-Pelvis Oncologic CT

Authors: S\'el\'ena Jean-Mactoux, Sophie Loizillon, Ghina Jardali, Amandine Cromb\'e, Nathalie Lassau, Alexandre B\^one

Abstract: Objectives: Radiologists interpreting oncologic chest-abdomen-pelvis (CAP) CT must also detect non-oncologic conditions, yet existing CT foundation models are trained on single anatomical regions or on non-oncologic data. We developed and evaluated CAPTOR, a vision-language foundation model for oncologic CAP CT that uses an anatomically structured preprocessing strategy to learn from whole examinations. Materials & Methods: CAPTOR is a contrastive vision-language model pretrained on 25,512 predominantly oncologic CAP CT-report pairs (single or multiple series) from a retrospective multicenter dataset (North America, Europe, Asia). It was compared with five CT foundation models, a larger-scale variant (CAPTOR-100K) and an ablation without anatomical preprocessing (CAPTOR-NoPre) on classification and retrieval using held-out internal and public test sets, and on survival prediction using TumorImagingBench. Confidence intervals and pairwise comparisons used patient-level bootstrap and permutation tests. Results: On 10,794 test studies, CAPTOR achieved higher AUC than all five models on CAP (0.767 vs. 0.695 for the next-best model; p<0.001) and oncologic subsets (0.784 vs. 0.734; p<0.001), with superior retrieval on both, and ranked first on three of four oncologic survival benchmarks. Despite substantially fewer training examinations, it performed comparably to CAPTOR-100K. Removing the anatomically structured preprocessing reduced classification performance, including on the institutionally held-out test subset (macro-AUC 0.737 vs. 0.691 for CAPTOR-NoPre, p<0.01). Conclusion: Anatomically structured preprocessing enabled whole-examination CAP CT interpretation, including multiple-series acquisitions, and improved downstream performance. CAPTOR outperformed state-of-the-art CT foundation models on CAP and oncologic subsets while matching a substantially larger-scale variant.

replace Spectral World Renderer: Unified 3D Hyperspectral Unmixing

Authors: Chia-Ming Lee, Yu-Jou Hsiao, Ming-Ching Chang, Xin Li, Yu-Lun Liu, Chih-Chung Hsu

Abstract: Hyperspectral images (HSI) preserve material evidence lost in RGB, yet 3D material identification from multi-view HSI faces two obstacles. Spectral Gaussian splatting reconstructs spectra accurately but leaves unmixing to per-pixel post-processing, while multi-view hyperspectral capture is costly and real datasets lack pixel-level material labels and sub-pixel proportions. We propose Spectral World Renderer (SWR), a material-aware explicit representation that unifies 3D spectral reconstruction and unmixing: it fits a Gaussian scaffold from RGB and decodes ray-Gaussian intersections into abundances over a shared endmember dictionary. Additionally, appearance guidance prevents spectrally similar materials from merging when intensity absorbs their brightness differences. Moreover, SWR requires no material-label supervision during training. To address the data gap, we further construct the SWR Benchmark (SWR-Bench): a coding-agent pipeline generates eight indoor and outdoor worlds from measured reflectance, aligning HSI with both annotations. SWR achieves the highest mIoU in all eight worlds and the highest mean PSNR on each real dataset. On an 11-class urban world, it trains at least 3.67x faster than unmixing baselines; rendering averages 34.9 joint spectral and label views/s over four worlds on a single RTX 4090.

replace FlashReFocus: Interactive Image Refocusing in Seconds

Authors: Ching-Heng Cheng, Chia-Ming Lee, Ming-Ching Chang, Xin Li, Chih-Chung Hsu

Abstract: Single-image refocusing changes the focal plane and depth of field of a photograph after capture. Users compare focal choices on one photograph, so content stability across edits matters alongside quality and speed. Recent generative methods rerun large diffusion models for every edit, even when they first restore an all-in-focus image, so content can change inconsistently between edits. Yet only this restoration is highly ill-posed and benefits from a generative prior, whereas rendering blur is largely determined by depth and the focal plane. We therefore propose FlashReFocus, which runs diffusion once per photograph and renders each edit with a lightweight network. A one-step diffusion deblurrer with a decoder refiner recovers fine detail efficiently while balancing fidelity and perceptual quality. The renderer uses an explicit defocus map for control and defocus-bias attention to promote information exchange between tokens with similar blur magnitudes. Because every edit reuses the same reconstruction, in-focus content stays consistent across edits, and editing the map enables tilt-shift and two-plane effects. We further collect 3CReal, a benchmark of 89 pairs from three camera-lens systems, to evaluate generalization to unseen optics. For zero-shot bokeh rendering on EBB! Val176 and 3CReal, FlashReFocus leads the evaluated methods in fidelity and perceptual quality. At 1536x1536, a complete refocus takes 2.45 s and each subsequent edit 0.46 s on one RTX PRO 6000.

replace Tempo-Zero: Recursive Self-Play in Executable Worlds for Video Reasoning

Authors: Jingmin Zhu, Yanyan Zou, Xiuyu Li, James Bailey, Dinh Phung, Tao Yu, Yunyun Hou, Jun Liu, Jun Zhou, Sirui Wang, Nan Duan, Jiachen Liu, Qiuhong Ke

Abstract: Video reasoning requires understanding how events unfold over time, yet videos with accurate temporal annotations are expensive to collect. Self-play offers an alternative by letting a model generate and solve its own tasks without manual annotation. However, existing video self-play methods train on fixed sets of recorded videos, so they cannot alter the underlying event dynamics and rely on noisy pseudo-labels derived from the model's own outputs, such as majority voting, that risk reinforcing shared model errors. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Tempo-Zero, a self-improvement framework that closes a loop of executable environments, execution-verified self-play, and environment evolution, where executing a program renders a video with the ground-truth option and interval of every question. A single policy plays both Questioner and Solver, proposing questions and answering them with timestamped evidence lines, and is trained on execution-grounded rewards instead of model consensus. The Solver's performance steers a curriculum over tasks and difficulty levels, and the policy selects rule changes that enrich the event dynamics of later rounds. Despite being trained solely on simple programs, without supervised fine-tuning or external video data which prior methods typically require, Tempo-Zero outperforms the base models and recent self-play baselines on average across twelve real-world video benchmarks at different model scales, with particularly strong gains on temporal grounding tasks. Project page: https://alchemist-jo.github.io/tempo-zero/

URLs: https://alchemist-jo.github.io/tempo-zero/

replace TurboWAM: Learning What Matters for Fast World Action Models

Authors: Guile Wu, Shuangzhi Li, Xingxin Chen, Chengjie Huang, Sam Hosseini, Tongtong Cao, Dongfeng Bai, Yingxue Zhang

Abstract: World action models have demonstrated promising performance in robot policy learning, yet their high inference latency remains a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. Recent approaches have sought to improve inference efficiency by eliminating test-time future-frame imagination or accelerating action prediction. However, aggregating representations from all video world model layers can introduce redundant computation, while standard backpropagation may distribute action-supervision gradients unevenly across layers, limiting the effectiveness of world representation learning. In this work, we propose TurboWAM, a fast world action model that learns to efficiently aggregate and optimize world representations for action prediction. Specifically, TurboWAM introduces an efficient world representation aggregation mechanism that restricts aggregation to a bounded range of relevant world model layers identified through layer relevance analysis. Within this bounded range, TurboWAM estimates aggregation weights for different world model layers based on the current action denoising state and combines them with learnable layer-wise weights to fuse world representations. Moreover, TurboWAM employs a world model gradient modulation mechanism that decomposes action-supervision gradients into a layer-shared component and layer-specific residual components, enabling more balanced gradient propagation across world model layers. This mechanism operates exclusively during training and introduces no additional inference computation. Together, these designs enable more efficient world representation learning and low-latency action prediction. Extensive experiments on multiple robot manipulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks show that TurboWAM achieves low inference latency while maintaining competitive task success rates compared with state-of-the-art methods.

replace PhoneValue-Bench: A Dual-Ground-Truth Scorecard Benchmark for Smartphone Recommerce Inspection

Authors: Zihan Deng, Chuanzhi Xu, Junjie Huang, Xiaozhen Zhong, Du Tiantian

Abstract: Remote smartphone recommerce is scaling quickly, and locking a repurchase offer from seller photos and diagnostic screens before the handset arrives has become a central operational task. Existing multimodal resources mainly target defect localization, catalog attributes, or screen reading. They overlook payout-facing scorecards with dual cosmetic and operational ground truth under a declared input contract. This gap stems from two challenges: (1) the lack of a large weakly labeled trade-in corpus kept separate from a compact audited ranking cut, and (2) architectures that either flatten unordered photo bags into one prompt or collapse evaluation into a single price scalar. To address these challenges, we propose PhoneValue-Bench, a dual-ground-truth recommerce framework with PhoneValue-Corpus for weak training on 21,550 orders and 278,748 images, and PV-Rank as the only leaderboard on 335 quality-gated orders. Systems are ranked by CardScore under LeadGate, with Cosmetic Fill and Operational Fill as diagnostics, while grade and price stay off the board. Notably, ValueAgent is a six-stage perception-fusion reference that canonicalizes views, detects regional defects, reads screenshots, and fuses evidence under warehouse-style rules, so bag-of-images prompting is not the only evaluation unit. On the same PV-Rank cut and frozen scorer, this full-system and input-contract comparison finds ValueAgent at 84.1 CardScore against the strongest Flat Vision-Language Model at 69.9, with the largest reported gap in CosFill, while OpsFill remains limited under the two declared contracts. CardScore measures field-fill completeness, not quote accuracy.

replace GSExplorer: Interactive World Generation with a Persistent 3DGS World State

Authors: Sicheng Yu, Liu Liu, Xinjie Wang, Xinjiang Wang, Taojun Ding, Wenhao Hu, Beizhen Zhao, Puxin Yan, Zhizhong Su, Hao Wang

Abstract: Interactive world generation requires both accurate local control and stable scene consistency over long rollouts. Existing methods improve local generation through camera or geometry conditioning and extend historical context with memory mechanisms. However, long-horizon exploration remains challenging due to incomplete target-view guidance and inconsistencies accumulated during scene updates. We present \textbf{GSExplorer}, a generation--reconstruction framework built around a persistent 3D Gaussian world state. At each step, the current 3DGS scene provides multimodal rendering conditions for a video generator, while camera geometry is injected through a separate Relative Ray Encoding branch. Generated videos are then fed back to a SLAM-based reconstruction backend, where appearance and geometric consistency checks produce a shared support mask for selective scene updates and cross-chunk pose refinement. We further design a data-construction pipeline that reproduces realistic 3DGS degradation during scene extrapolation, yielding 293K training pairs of degraded multimodal renderings and real target videos. GSExplorer achieves the best overall performance among the compared methods on both single-step and three-step WorldScore evaluation, including a Camera Control score of 99.07 in single-step extrapolation. On 481-frame leave-and-return rollouts, it also shows strong revisit consistency.

replace OctreeSplat: Hierarchical Octree-Guided Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting

Authors: Yibin Zhao, Yihan Pan, Wenli Yang, Jun Nan, Jianjun Yi

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has shown considerable potential for novel view synthesis (NVS) and 3D reconstruction. Feed-forward methods avoid costly per-scene optimization, but fixed image or spatial resolutions limit their ability to allocate primitives according to local complexity. We present OctreeSplat, a hierarchical octree-guided feed-forward Gaussian splatting. OctreeSplat extracts multi-view features and geometric priors from a frozen visual foundation model (VFM), constructs a hierarchical octree, and predicts densification scores. The resulting representation allocates Gaussian primitives at spatially varying densities according to local complexity. Stochastic solids volume rendering improves depth rendering, strengthens geometric supervision, and supports surface reconstruction. When camera parameters are available, an optional test-time optimization (TTO) module improves agreement between predicted and ground-truth parameters. Training updates only 76 million additional parameters and requires 35 GPU-hours. Experiments on Replica, TUM, Tanks and Temples, and DTU demonstrate competitive RGB view synthesis, depth synthesis, and surface reconstruction. OctreeSplat reduces the number of Gaussian primitives while maintaining high reconstruction quality.

replace VidDub: Replacement-Aware Supervision for Two-Step Video Dubbing

Authors: Yexin Liu, Yunlong Lin, Zehan Wang, Guangxin He, Xinli Xu, Jiajun Zha, Yaofu Liu, Yueze Wang, Bowen Ping, Yuanming Li, Amie Wang, Bernard Ghanem, Bing Li, Harry Yang

Abstract: Video dubbing aims to synthesize lip movements that match target speech while preserving the speaker identity, head pose, and background of a source video. Existing editors are predominantly trained via self-reconstruction under original paired audio, even though the source mouth becomes a conflicting target when driving speech is replaced. We introduce \emph{replacement-aware cross-audio supervision}: paired-audio samples use full-frame reconstruction, whereas unpaired replacement-audio samples mask out source-mouth reconstruction losses and supervise the mouth solely through audio--visual synchronization while preserving unmasked facial regions and background. Because offline dubbing provides the complete source video and target speech beforehand, we present \method, a full-context diffusion editor that uses matched trajectory regression to transfer CFG-guided teacher trajectories to a two-step student and distribution matching to recover visual quality after compression. We will release the curated training dataset, VidDub-Bench, fixed paired- and cross-audio evaluation manifests, preprocessing outputs, and unified evaluation code. Across three training seeds, masking out conflicting source-mouth reconstruction improves cross-audio Sync-C by 0.833. At two NFE, the final student provides the strongest cross-audio synchronization and visual-speech recognition among the evaluated methods, with competitive visual fidelity and source preservation.

replace Integrating Learned Priors through Prior-Space Reparameterization for Physically Based Inverse Rendering

Authors: Hun Hwangbo, Minjun Park, Yongsung Kim, Wooseok Song, Jungmin Ko, Junghyun Hwang, Sungroh Yoon

Abstract: Physically based inverse rendering is an ambiguous problem, allowing independently optimized material variables to absorb appearance variations caused by illumination. Learned material predictions provide useful cues about which surface points have similar materials. Existing approaches often incorporate these cues through auxiliary regularization losses. Instead, we propose a novel prior-guided reparameterization of the material optimization space. We fuse multi-view material predictions onto a mesh and construct a permutohedral lattice in the resulting prior feature space. Surface points with similar predictions share lattice variables, restricting the material field to a prior-defined subspace that is optimized jointly with geometry and illumination using a purely photometric objective. Experiments on three synthetic benchmarks demonstrate improved material decomposition and relighting, with results on par with the strongest baseline on Stanford-ORB. As a complete system, our method improves relighting PSNR by 1.4-1.5\,dB over the strongest evaluated mesh-based baseline on Synthetic4Relight and TensoIR-Synthetic. Analysis of the optimization shows that shared variables pool per-vertex photometric gradients into updates that are more consistent across sampled views. In a controlled comparison within the same pipeline, the restricted subspace, despite its reduced degrees of freedom, yields more accurate material decomposition than both unrestricted per-vertex optimization and a regularization loss built from the same prior.

replace GenRA: Ultra-Low-Bitrate Generative Video Compression via Representation Adaptation

Authors: Jiancheng Zhao, Jixiang Luo, Haibin Huang, Chi Zhang, Xuelong Li

Abstract: Preserving fine visual details in ultra-low-bitrate video compression remains challenging due to the loss of critical information. Recent generative video compression methods exploit generative priors to improve perceptual quality. These pretrained generative representations are generally not optimized for compression. Some approaches therefore use an additional learned codec to map them into a separate latent space for compression. To this end, we propose GenRA, which adapts pretrained video representations for direct quantization and entropy coding without an additional learned codec. Specifically, we use vector quantization to adapt a pretrained video VAE into a discrete video tokenizer. A lightweight categorical autoregressive entropy model combines local and global context to model the probabilities of the resulting token indices. We jointly optimize the tokenizer and entropy model using a differentiable rate surrogate, allowing the encoder to adjust token assignments based on coding cost and reconstruction quality. Experiments on UVG, MCL-JCV, and HEVC Class B show average bitrate savings of 84.1% and 82.3% over GLC-Video in terms of LPIPS and DISTS, respectively.

replace EgoGeneralist: Unifying Egocentric Action-Video Tasks through Video-Native Modeling

Authors: Yanlin Jin, Bowen Yang, Cheng Zhang, Yifan Yang, Zhe Li, Jianfei Cai, Kai Zhu

Abstract: Modern video models possess strong visual priors, yet do not natively represent human action in metric 3D space. We present EgoGeneralist, which adapts pretrained video generators to unify action-conditioned video generation, pose estimation, and joint action-video generation for egocentric manipulation. Our role-adaptive action renderer provides video-native representations for multidirectional action-video learning, making metric motion usable as both a spatial control signal and a recoverable generation target. Cross-stream action-video coupling establishes close correspondence between metric motion and visual dynamics through lightweight adaptation while preserving the pretrained visual prior. Together, these designs establish a generalist framework spanning six action-video tasks. With only narrow-domain data, EgoGeneralist generalizes to diverse unseen scenes and interactions with effective action control and visual quality, bridging pretrained visual knowledge and metric 3D human motion.

replace Look Closer, Align Better: Explicit Visual Attributes for Robust EEG-to-Image Decoding

Authors: Ke Xiao, Banghua Yang, Wenbin Guo, Fenqi Rong

Abstract: EEG-to-image retrieval commonly aligns neural responses with representations from pretrained vision encoders. However, these representations may underrepresent fine-grained visual details needed to distinguish semantically similar images. To address this limitation, we first investigate the EEG decodability and complementary predictive value of explicit visual attributes, identifying visual cues that capture EEG-relevant information beyond pretrained representations. Building on these findings, we propose AttrEcho, a framework that enriches pretrained visual representations with complementary, spatially aligned visual attributes. Specifically, Region-Aligned Visual Attribute Fusion (RAVF) integrates EEG-relevant local visual attributes with spatially corresponding pretrained features through a learnable signed residual gate. We further employ Feature Token Aggregation (FTA), which learns dimension-specific weights across regions to aggregate refined visual features for contrastive alignment with EEG representations. Experiments on THINGS-EEG demonstrate that AttrEcho achieves 94.8% Top-1 and 99.7% Top-5 accuracy in intra-subject 200-way retrieval, while maintaining 82.1% Top-1 accuracy as the gallery expands to 1,600 images with same-concept distractors. AttrEcho also achieves 25.3% Top-1 and 53.0% Top-5 accuracy in inter-subject retrieval. These results highlight the importance of EEG-relevant visual attributes and spatially aligned fusion for fine-grained EEG-to-image retrieval.

replace FusionCLIP: Multimodal Contrastive Pretraining for Fusion Plasma Diagnostics

Authors: Qiang Chen, Xiao Wang, Qingquan Yang, Hao Si, Zikang Yan, Guosheng Xu, Jin Tang

Abstract: Visible-light videos and multivariate diagnostic signals provide complementary observations of tokamak discharges. However, existing fusion pretraining methods primarily learn representations by reconstructing or predicting observations, leaving the correspondence between these modalities underexplored. Representation learning is further challenged by spatially localized plasma regions in video frames and substantial differences in diagnostic sampling rates. We propose FusionCLIP, a multimodal contrastive pretraining framework. A selective video encoder retains informative visual tokens, while a query-based time-series encoder integrates asynchronous diagnostic signals using their observation timestamps. Contrastive learning aligns representations of the two modalities. We construct the EAST Multimodal Diagnostics (EAST-MMD) dataset, comprising 1307 EAST discharges, and evaluate the model through independent fine-tuning on three tasks: disruption prediction, edge-localized mode (ELM) classification, and H98 regression. FusionCLIP achieves a Joint score of 89.07%, with absolute gains of 2.41% and 2.90% over the best competing baseline, and the in-domain contrastive pretraining baseline CLIP-Base, respectively. Ablations and attention visualizations further support the contributions of its key components.

replace MotionGraphicsBench: Evaluating Motion Graphics Generation with Programmatic Verification

Authors: Jiaju Ma, Ashvini Kumar Jindal, Chuhua Wang, Sumithra Bhakthavatsalam

Abstract: Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, outperforming frontier VLM judges by 4.4%-12.8% while achieving at least 90% recall for both human approvals and rejections. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 5.0% to 68.0%. Across 15 models at their highest reasoning effort, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We fine-tune Qwen3.8-27B with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) using our verification programs as rewards, improving pass@1 from 30.31% to 52.19% on held-out instances. Our project page is at https://motion-graphics-bench.github.io/.

replace Graphene: A Lightweight Transformer with Ising Mean-Field Refinement for Image Super-Resolution

Authors: Seungki Hong, Taekwang Jang

Abstract: This paper proposes Graphene, a lightweight image super-resolution architecture with two complementary feature refinement mechanisms: Ising-model-inspired local feature refinement using an Ising Refinement Block (IRB) and stage-history-based feature refinement using a History Attention (HistAttn) module. The IRB reuses attention output features and pre-softmax attention logits to construct signed pairwise token couplings within each local window, applies unrolled mean-field updates, and refines features through a gated residual correction. These signed couplings allow alignment and opposition between latent spins to modulate local feature refinement. HistAttn attends over the current stage output and earlier representations at the same token position and adaptively blends the current stage output with a history-weighted feature aggregate without adding spatial pairwise attention. On Urban100 at $\times4$, Graphene and Graphene-light improve over IET and IET-light by 0.24 dB and 0.38 dB, respectively, with 34.3%/13.7% fewer parameters, 44.8%/44.4% fewer FLOPs, 83.9%/95.9% lower latency, and 63.7%/92.0% lower peak memory. Real-world SR comparisons further show that Graphene recovers fine details and preserves textures across diverse degradations. Code and reference checkpoints are available at https://github.com/skethz/Graphene
